\mathsf{U}(1)
gauge field
A=A_{\mu}(x)\,{\rm d}x^{\mu}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})
coupled to a massless
7
7

7

The zero mass restriction is done for simplicity only, in order to illustrate the general construction. There is no problem with including a mass term for the Dirac spinor as well, and indeed we shall do so later on in Section
4
.
Dirac spinor
\psi
on four-dimensional Minkowski spacetime
\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}
. We use the shorthand notation for partial derivatives
\partial_{\mu}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}
, where
x^{\mu}
are coordinates on
\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}
with
t=x^{0}
the time direction. The infinitesimal
\mathsf{U}(1)
gauge variations are given by

\displaystyle\delta_{c}A_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{e}\,\partial_{\mu}c\ ,\quad\delta_{c}\psi={\mathrm{i}}\,c\,\psi\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\delta_{c}\bar{\psi}=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\bar{\psi}\,c\ ,

where
c\in\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})
is the infinitesimal gauge parameter,
e
is the electric charge of the fermion, and
\bar{\psi}=\psi^{\dagger}\,\gamma^{0}
is the conjugate Dirac spinor with
\gamma^{\mu}
the Dirac matrices in four dimensions (see Appendix
B
for conventions and identities).

The action functional invariant under these gauge transformations is

\displaystyle S\big(A,\psi,\bar{\psi}\,\big)=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,{\rm d}^{4}x\ \Big(-\frac{1}{4}\,F^{\mu\nu}\,F_{\mu\nu}+\bar{\psi}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\big(\partial_{\mu}\psi-{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,A_{\mu}\,\psi\big)\Big)\ ,

(3.1)

where we introduced the field strength tensor
F={\rm d}A\in\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})
whose components are given by
F=\frac{1}{2}\,F_{\mu\nu}\,{\rm d}x^{\mu}\wedge{\rm d}x^{\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\,(\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu})\,{\rm d}x^{\mu}\wedge{\rm d}x^{\nu}
.
The corresponding equations of motion are

\displaystyle\partial_{\nu}F^{\mu\nu}=-e\,\bar{\psi}\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\psi\ ,\quad{\mathrm{i}}\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\big(\partial_{\mu}\psi-{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,A_{\mu}\,\psi\big)=0{\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad}\big(\partial_{\mu}\bar{\psi}+{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,\bar{\psi}\,A_{\mu}\big)\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\gamma^{\mu}=0\ .

Corresponding to the
\mathsf{U}(1)
gauge symmetry is the electric matter current
J=J_{\mu}\,{\rm d}x^{\mu}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})
associated with the Dirac spinor
\psi
given by

J^{\mu}=e\,\bar{\psi}\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\psi\ .

(3.2)

The gauge field
A
minimally couples to this current and the continuity equation
8
8

8

We use the notation
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi}}{{\approx}}
to indicate an equality which holds on-shell when the equation of motion for a field
\Phi
is imposed. When currents are conserved in this sense, we refer to them as ‘weakly conserved’.

\partial_{\mu}J^{\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\approx}}0
follows from the equation of motion for
A
.
It gives the conserved electric charge

Q_{B}=\int_{B}\,{\rm d}B\ J^{0}=e\,\int_{B}\,{\rm d}B\ \psi^{\dagger}\,\psi

(3.3)

enclosed by any spatial volume
B\subseteq\mathbbm{R}^{3}\subset\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}
at fixed time with volume form
{\rm d}B
. The conservation law together with Gauss’ theorem imply that the time variation of
Q_{B}
is cancelled by the net current through the spatial surface
\partial B
bounding the volume
B
.
9
9

9

A more general Lorentz invariant definition uses a codimension one hypersurface
B\subset\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}
. The corresponding charge
Q_{B}
is then conserved in the normal direction to
B
.

From the modern perspective of generalized global symmetries
[
38
]
, the conserved electric charge is a ‘zero-form symmetry’, which is implemented by codimension one defects
B
in the field theory, whose natural charged objects are the quanta of the local fermion field
\psi
. Electrodynamics also possesses a one-form magnetic symmetry, implemented by codimension two defects
\Sigma
, with two-form current
\widetilde{F}=\frac{1}{2}\,g\,\epsilon_{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}\,F^{\lambda\sigma}\,{\rm d}x^{\mu}\wedge{\rm d}x^{\nu}\in\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})
. Its conservation law
\partial_{\mu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}=0
is equivalent to the Bianchi identity for the field strength
F
, or alternatively to the absence of magnetic monopoles. The natural objects that are charged under this symmetry are the non-local ’t Hooft line defects, of magnetic charge
g
obeying the Dirac–Zwanziger quantization condition
e\,g\in 2\pi\,\mathbbm{Z}
which ensures mutual locality of the electric and magnetic charges. The corresponding conserved quantity is the magnetic flux

\displaystyle\varPhi_{\Sigma}=g\,\int_{\Sigma}\,F

(3.4)

through a spatial surface
\Sigma\subset\mathbbm{R}^{3}
. For an open surface, by Stokes’ theorem this is equivalently computed by the usual holonomy integral
\varPhi_{\Sigma}=g\,\int_{\partial\Sigma}\,A
over the loop
\partial\Sigma
bounding
\Sigma
. For a closed surface, the magnetic flux vanishes.

The global one-form symmetry can be gauged by minimally coupling the current
\widetilde{F}
to a background two-form field
b\in\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})
with the
\mathsf{U}(1)
gauge transformation
\delta_{\lambda}b={\rm d}\lambda
for
\lambda\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})
. This shifts (
3.1
) by the action functional of a
BF
-type topological field theory:

\displaystyle S_{b}\big(A,\psi,\bar{\psi}\,\big)=S\big(A,\psi,\bar{\psi}\,\big)-g\,\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,b\wedge F\ .

Integrating the source term by parts, the shifted action becomes

\displaystyle S_{b}\big(A,\psi,\bar{\psi}\,\big)=S\big(A,\psi,\bar{\psi}\,\big)-g\,\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,A\wedge{\rm d}b\ .

This shows that the field strength
H={\rm d}b\in\Omega^{3}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})
of the fixed external field
b
is electrically charged under the gauged zero-form symmetry, thereby introducing a background which modifies the matter current (
3.2
) to

\displaystyle J_{b}^{\mu}=q\,\bar{\psi}\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\psi+g\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}\,\partial_{\nu}b_{\lambda\sigma}\ .

By Gauss’ theorem, the electric charge (
3.3
) is then shifted to
Q_{B,b}=Q_{B}+g\,\int_{\partial B}\,b
by the flux of the two-form
b
through the boundary of the spatial volume
B
.

It will prove useful later on, in our general twist deformation formalism, to rewrite this well-known field theory in a basis independent form. Let
{\rm d}
be the exterior differential,
\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
the Hodge star operator induced by the Minkowski metric, and
\delta=\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,{\rm d}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
the corresponding codifferential.
Let us introduce a background field
V
defined by

V=\gamma_{\mu}\,\gamma_{\nu}\,\gamma_{\lambda}\,\gamma_{5}\,{\rm d}x^{\mu}\wedge{\rm d}x^{\nu}\wedge{\rm d}x^{\lambda}={\mathrm{i}}\,\epsilon_{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}\,\gamma^{\sigma}\,{\rm d}x^{\mu}\wedge{\rm d}x^{\nu}\wedge{\rm d}x^{\lambda}\ ,

(3.5)

where in the second equality we used the Dirac matrix identity (
B.1
).
This is a gauge invariant closed three-form valued in the endomorphism algebra
{\mathrm{End}}(\mathscr{S})
of the complex spinor representation
\mathscr{S}
of
\mathsf{Spin}(1,3)
(see Appendix
B
). It can be used to rewrite the spinor action as

S_{\psi}=-\frac{1}{6}\,\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,\bar{\psi}\,V\wedge({\rm d}\psi-{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,A\,\psi)\ .

(3.6)

Expanding all the forms,
one arrives at the usual covariant Dirac action
10
10

10

The covariant action for a Dirac spinor in four dimensions in an arbitrary basis is usually written as

S_{\psi}=-\frac{1}{6}\,\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,\bar{\psi}\,\gamma_{5}\,{\underline{e}}\wedge{\underline{e}}\wedge{\underline{e}}\wedge({\rm d}\psi-{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,A\,\psi)\ ,

where
{\underline{e}}={\underline{e}}^{a}_{\,\mu}\,\gamma_{a}\,{\rm d}x^{\mu}
is a vierbein one-form valued in
\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{S})

[
39
]
. This action can also describe a Dirac spinor in curved spacetime, where the gauge field
A
is then the spin connection
\omega
.
Since we work in a fixed background spacetime, we simply group the three flat vierbeins
{\underline{e}}\wedge{\underline{e}}\wedge{\underline{e}}
and the chirality matrix
\gamma_{5}
into a single background field
V
.

S_{\psi}=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,{\rm d}^{4}x\ \bar{\psi}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\gamma^{\mu}\,(\partial_{\mu}\psi-{\mathrm{i}}\,q\,A_{\mu}\,\psi)\ .

The full set of equations of motion can now be written in the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}F_{A}&=\delta\,{\rm d}A-\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}}{6}\,e\,\bar{\psi}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}V\,\psi=0\ ,\\[4.0pt]
F_{\bar{\psi}}&=-\tfrac{1}{6}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big(V\wedge({\rm d}\psi-{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,A\,\psi)\big)=0\ ,\\[4.0pt]
F_{\psi}&=-\tfrac{1}{6}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big(({\rm d}\bar{\psi}+{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,\bar{\psi}\,A)\wedge V\big)=0\ .\end{split}

(3.7)

3.2
L_{\infty}
-algebra of electrodynamics

The graded vector space
L=L^{0}\oplus L^{1}\oplus L^{2}\oplus L^{3}
of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra underlying electrodynamics is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}L^{0}=L^{3}&=\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})\ ,\\[4.0pt]
L^{1}=L^{2}=\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})\,&\oplus\,\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathscr{S})\,\oplus\,\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathscr{S})\ .\end{split}

(3.8)

We arrange the physical fields
{\cal A}\in L^{1}
and their duals
\mathcal{A}^{+}\in L^{2}
as

{\cal A}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}A\\
{\psi}\\
\bar{\psi}\end{array}\right){\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad}\mathcal{A}^{+}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}A^{+}\\
\bar{{\psi}}^{+}\\
{\psi}^{+}\end{array}\right)\ .

(3.9)

The elements
\mathcal{A}^{+}
correspond to antifields in the BV formalism containing the equations of motion
F_{\cal A}=0
, where

\displaystyle F_{\cal A}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}F_{A}\\
F_{{\psi}}\\
F_{\bar{\psi}}\end{array}\right)\ .

Elements
c\in L^{0}
correspond to ghosts in the BV–BRST formalism related to the gauge parameters, while elements
c^{+}\in L^{3}
correspond to their antifields containing the Noether identities
{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathcal{A}}F_{\mathcal{A}}=0
.

The differential
\ell_{1}
is given by

\ell_{1}(c)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\tfrac{1}{e}\,{\rm d}c\\[4.30554pt]
0\\
0\end{array}\right)\ ,\quad\ell_{1}({\cal A})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\delta\,{\rm d}A\\
\tfrac{1}{6}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}({\rm d}\bar{\psi}\wedge V)\\[4.30554pt]
-\tfrac{1}{6}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}(V\wedge{\rm d}\psi)\end{array}\right){\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad}\ell_{1}({\cal A}^{+})=\tfrac{1}{e}\,\delta A^{+}\ .

(3.10)

The non-vanishing
2
-brackets are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{2}(c,&\,{\cal A})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
{\mathrm{i}}\,c\,\psi\\
-{\mathrm{i}}\,\bar{\psi}\,c\end{array}\right)\quad,\quad\ell_{2}(c,{\cal A}^{+})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
-{\mathrm{i}}\,\bar{\psi}^{+}\,c\\
{\mathrm{i}}\,c\,{{\psi}}^{+}\end{array}\right)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell_{2}({\cal A}_{1},{\cal A}_{2})=\tfrac{1}{6}\,e\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}&\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathrm{i}}\,\bar{\psi}_{1}\,V\,\psi_{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\,\bar{\psi}_{2}\,V\,\psi_{1}\\
-\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\bar{\psi}_{1}\,A_{2}\wedge V-{\mathrm{i}}\,\bar{\psi}_{2}\,A_{1}\wedge V\\
-\,{\mathrm{i}}\,V\wedge A_{1}\,\psi_{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\,V\wedge A_{2}\,\psi_{1}\end{array}\right)\quad,\quad\ell_{2}({\cal A},{\cal A}^{+})={\mathrm{i}}\,\bar{\psi}^{+}\,\psi+{\mathrm{i}}\,\bar{\psi}\,{{\psi}}^{+}\ .\end{split}

(3.11)

All higher brackets
\ell_{n}
with
n\geq 3
vanish.
We checked explicitly that these brackets satisfy the homotopy relations, and thus define an
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathscr{L}
(which is a differential graded Lie algebra in this case).

The underlying cochain complex of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathscr{L}
is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})[-2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})[-3]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{matrix}\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}},\mathscr{S})[-1]\\[4.30554pt]
\oplus\\[4.30554pt]
\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}},\mathscr{S})[-1]\end{matrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{matrix}\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}},\mathscr{S})[-2]\\[4.30554pt]
\oplus\\[4.30554pt]
\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}},\mathscr{S})[-2]\end{matrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{1}{e}\,{\rm d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta\,{\rm d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{1}{e}\,\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ {\ \Big(\begin{matrix}0\!\!&\!\!{\mathrm{i}}\overset{\leftarrow}{\not{\partial}}\\[-0.90417pt]
{\mathrm{i}}\,\not{\partial}\!\!&\!\!0\end{matrix}\Big)\ }\ }

(3.12)

where
{\mathrm{i}}\,\overset{\leftarrow}{\not{\partial}}\,\bar{\psi}:=\frac{1}{6}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}({\rm d}\bar{\psi}\wedge V)
and
{\mathrm{i}}\,\not{\partial}\,\psi:=-\frac{1}{6}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}(V\wedge{\rm d}\psi)
.
The cohomology
H^{\bullet}(L)
of this complex is given by the free fields in the kernel of
\ell_{1}
. This defines the minimal model for the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathscr{L}

[
3
,
9
]
, which is an
L_{\infty}
-algebra
H^{\bullet}(\mathscr{L})
quasi-isomorphic to
\mathscr{L}
with underlying cochain complex
\big(H^{\bullet}(L),0\big)
. In particular, fields
(A^{\textrm{\tiny(0)}},\psi^{\textrm{\tiny(0)}},\bar{\psi}{}^{\textrm{\tiny(0)}})
in the degree
1
cohomology

\displaystyle H^{1}(L)=\frac{\ker(\delta\,{\rm d})}{{\rm im}({\rm d})}\ \oplus\ \ker\bigg(\begin{matrix}0&{\mathrm{i}}\,\overset{\leftarrow}{\not{\partial}}\,\\
{\mathrm{i}}\,\not{\partial}&0\end{matrix}\bigg)

correspond to the usual photon states
A^{\textrm{\tiny(0)}}
of free Maxwell theory on
{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}
and the conventional description of free spinors as states
(\psi^{\textrm{\tiny(0)}},\bar{\psi}{}^{\textrm{\tiny(0)}})
satisfying the Dirac equation.

One easily checks that the higher brackets of the
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathscr{L}
reproduce the gauge transformations, equations of motion and Noether identity for classical electrodynamics according to the prescription of Section
2.1
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta_{c}{\cal A}&=\ell_{1}({c})+\ell_{2}(c,{\cal A})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{1}{e}\,{\rm d}c\\[4.30554pt]
{\mathrm{i}}\,c\,\psi\\
-{\mathrm{i}}\,\bar{\psi}\,c\end{array}\right)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
F_{\cal A}&=\ell_{1}({\cal A})-\frac{1}{2}\,\ell_{2}({\cal A},{\cal A})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\delta\,{\rm d}A-\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}}{6}\,e\,\bar{\psi}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}V\,\psi\\[4.30554pt]
\tfrac{1}{6}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big(({\rm d}\bar{\psi}+{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,\bar{\psi}\,A)\wedge V\big)\\[4.30554pt]
-\tfrac{1}{6}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big(V\wedge({\rm d}\psi-{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,A\,\psi)\big)\end{array}\right)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathcal{A}}F_{\cal A}&=\ell_{1}(F_{\cal A})-\ell_{2}({\cal A},F_{\cal A})=\tfrac{1}{e}\,\delta F_{A}+{\mathrm{i}}\,\bar{\psi}\,F_{\bar{\psi}}-{\mathrm{i}}\,F_{\psi}\,\psi\ .\end{split}

(3.13)

The Noether identity
{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathcal{A}}F_{\cal A}=0
can be understood in different ways. Following
[
40
]
we interpret it as reproducing the weakly conserved matter current in basis independent form. Indeed, when the equation of motion
F_{A}=0
for the gauge field is inserted, it gives

-\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}}{6}\,e\,\bar{\psi}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big(V\wedge({\rm d}\psi-{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,A\,\psi)\big)-\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}}{6}\,e\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big(({\rm d}\bar{\psi}+{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,\bar{\psi}\,A)\wedge V\big)\psi=-\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}}{6}\,e\,\delta\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big(\bar{\psi}\,V\,\psi\big)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\approx}}\ 0\ .

The
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathscr{L}
is naturally endowed with a cyclic structure defined by the non-vanishing inner products

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle c,c^{+}\rangle=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{3,1}}\,c\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}c^{+}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\langle{\cal A},{\cal A}^{+}\rangle=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,A\wedge\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,A^{+}-\bar{\psi}^{+}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\psi+\bar{\psi}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}{{\psi}}^{+}\ .\end{split}

(3.14)

It is then straightforward to verify that the corresponding homotopy Maurer–Cartan action functional for
\mathscr{L}
coincides with the action functional of electrodynamics:

\displaystyle\begin{split}S(\mathcal{A})&=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\langle{\cal A},\ell_{1}({\cal A})\rangle-\tfrac{1}{6}\,\langle{\cal A},\ell_{2}({\cal A},{\cal A})\rangle\\[4.0pt]
&=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,\frac{1}{2}\,F\wedge*_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,F-\frac{1}{12}\,\bar{\psi}\,V\wedge\big({\rm d}\psi-{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,A\,\psi\big)-\frac{1}{12}\,\big({\rm d}\bar{\psi}+{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,\bar{\psi}\,A\big)\wedge V\,\psi\\[4.0pt]
&=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,\frac{1}{2}\,F\wedge*_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,F-\frac{1}{6}\,\bar{\psi}\,V\wedge\big({\rm d}\psi-{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,A\,\psi\big)\ ,\end{split}

(3.15)

where we integrated by parts.

3.3
Braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra of electrodynamics

Following the steps described in Section
2.2
, we now deform the classical
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathscr{L}
of electrodynamics to a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathscr{L}^{\star}
. Like the scalar field theory of Section
2.3
, electrodynamics is only relativistically invariant, so we will work with an abelian Killing twist
\mathcal{F}
; that is, the vector fields entering the definition of
\mathcal{F}
are commuting Killing vectors for Minkowski spacetime
\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}
. The vector spaces (
3.8
) are then modules for the universal enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{iso}(1,3)=\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}\rtimes\mathfrak{so}(1,3)
.
In our present basis independent formulation, however, we need to further restrict to the isometries of
\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}
that preserve the closed three-form
V\in\Omega^{3}\big(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3},\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{S})\big)
, in order to ensure that the brackets (
3.10
) and (
3.11
) are equivariant maps.

Using the Dirac matrix identities from Appendix
B
, it is a straightforward calculation to show that the only Poincaré transformations which leave
V
invariant are translations
\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}\subset\mathfrak{iso}(1,3)
. This forces us to work in a suitably chosen local frame where the abelian twist reduces to the Moyal–Weyl twist (
2.26
)
[
41
]
; then the actions of the twist and of the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix on the background field
V
are trivial. The typical example, which we will eventually restrict to later on, is the Moyal–Weyl twist itself, which acts trivially on basis vector fields of the holonomic coordinate frame
\partial_{\mu}
and on basis one-forms of the dual coframe
{\rm d}x^{\mu}
; in particular, the star-products among basis one-forms, as well as between functions and basis one-forms, are trivial in this case.

The underlying graded vector space of the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathscr{L}^{\star}
is again given by (
3.8
), with the same cochain complex (
3.12
). The non-trivial
2
-brackets are modified to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell^{\star}_{2}(c,{\cal A})=&\,\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
{\mathrm{i}}\,c\star\psi\\
-{\mathrm{i}}\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi})\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(c)\end{array}\right)\qquad,\qquad\ell^{\star}_{2}(c,{\cal A}^{+})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
-{\mathrm{i}}\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi}^{+})\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(c)\\
{\mathrm{i}}\,c\star{{\psi}}^{+}\end{array}\right)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell^{\star}_{2}({\cal A}_{1},{\cal A}_{2})=&\,\tfrac{1}{6}\,e\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathrm{i}}\,\bar{\psi}_{1}\star V\star\psi_{2}+\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi}_{2})\star V\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\psi_{1})\\
-\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\bar{\psi}_{1}\star A_{2}\wedge_{\star}V-\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi}_{2})\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(A_{1})\wedge_{\star}V\\
-\,{\mathrm{i}}\,V\wedge_{\star}A_{1}\star\psi_{2}-{\mathrm{i}}\,V\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(A_{2})\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\psi_{1})\end{array}\right)\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\ell^{\star}_{2}({\cal A},{\cal A}^{+})=&\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi}^{+})\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\psi)+{\mathrm{i}}\,\bar{\psi}\star{{\psi}}^{+}\ .\end{split}

(3.16)

By construction, the braided homotopy relations (in this case the braided graded Jacobi identities) are satisfied.

Finally, the cyclic inner product on
\mathscr{L}^{\star}
is defined by the non-trivial pairings

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle c,c^{+}\rangle_{\star}=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,c\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,c^{+}\quad,\quad\langle{\cal A},{\cal A}^{+}\rangle_{\star}=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,A\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,A^{+}-\bar{\psi}^{+}\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\psi+\bar{\psi}\wedge_{\star}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,{{\psi}}^{+}\ .\end{split}

(3.17)

3.4
Braided electrodynamics

The braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathscr{L}^{\star}
defined by (
3.12
) and (
3.16
) defines a new noncommutative deformation of classical electrodynamics that we call ‘braided electrodynamics’. The braided
\mathsf{U}(1)
gauge transformations follow from

\delta^{\star}_{c}{\cal A}=\ell_{1}({c})+\ell^{\star}_{2}(c,{\cal A})

and are given by

\displaystyle\delta^{\star}_{c}A

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{e}\,{\rm d}c\ ,

(3.18)

\displaystyle\delta^{\star}_{c}\psi={\mathrm{i}}\,c\star\psi\quad,\quad

\displaystyle\delta^{\star}_{c}\bar{\psi}=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi})\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(c)\ .

(3.19)

Note that in the case of braided
\mathsf{U}(1)
gauge symmetry, the braided Lie algebra brackets also vanish. Therefore the braided
\mathsf{U}(1)
gauge theory retains its abelian nature (
3.18
). This is very different from the usual noncommutative deformation of
\mathsf{U}(1)
gauge theory with star-gauge symmetry (see e.g.
[
16
,
42
]
), which becomes a non-abelian gauge theory after deformation.

The equations of motion
F_{\mathcal{A}}^{\star}=0
of braided electrodynamics follow from braided homotopy Maurer–Cartan equations

F^{\star}_{\cal A}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}F^{\star}_{A}\\
F^{\star}_{{\psi}}\\
F^{\star}_{\bar{\psi}}\end{array}\right)=\ell_{1}({\cal A})-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\ell^{\star}_{2}({\cal A},{\cal A})\ ,

since all higher brackets vanish. Inserting the corresponding brackets given in (
3.10
) and (
3.16
), these equations can be written as

\displaystyle F^{\star}_{A}

\displaystyle=\delta\,{\rm d}A-\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}}{12}\,e\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big(\bar{\psi}\star V\star\psi+\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi})\star V\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\psi)\big)\ ,

\displaystyle F^{\star}_{\psi}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{6}\,\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big[\big({\rm d}\bar{\psi}+\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}}{2}\,e\,\bar{\psi}\star A+\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}}{2}\,e\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi})\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(A)\big)\wedge_{\star}V\big]=\tfrac{1}{12}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big[\big(D^{\mbox{\tiny L}}\bar{\psi}+D^{\mbox{\tiny R}}\bar{\psi}\big)\wedge_{\star}V\big]\ ,

\displaystyle F^{\star}_{\bar{\psi}}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{12}\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big[V\wedge_{\star}\big(D^{\mbox{\tiny L}}\psi+D^{\mbox{\tiny R}}\psi\big)\big]\ ,

where we introduced left and right braided covariant derivatives

D^{\mbox{\tiny L}}\psi={\rm d}\psi-{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,A\star\psi{\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad}D^{\mbox{\tiny R}}\psi={\rm d}\psi-{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(A)\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\psi)={\rm d}\psi-{\mathrm{i}}\,e\,\psi\star A\ .

Using the braided Leibniz rule

\delta^{\star}_{c}(\bar{\psi}\star V\star\psi)=\delta^{\star}_{c}\bar{\psi}\star V\star\psi+\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi})\star V\star\delta^{\star}_{\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(c)}\psi

one can easily check that the noncommutative fermion bilinear observable
\bar{\psi}\star V\star\psi
is invariant under braided gauge transformations and that

\displaystyle\delta^{\star}_{c}D^{\mbox{\tiny L,R}}\psi={\mathrm{i}}\,c\star D^{\mbox{\tiny L,R}}\psi\ .

Furthermore, one can explicitly show that all equations of motion transform covariantly, as expected on general grounds from the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra formulation, namely

\displaystyle\delta^{\star}_{c}F_{A}^{\star}=0\ ,\quad\delta^{\star}_{c}F^{\star}_{\psi}=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(F^{\star}_{\psi})\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(c)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\delta^{\star}_{c}F_{\bar{\psi}}^{\star}={\mathrm{i}}\,c\star F_{\bar{\psi}}^{\star}\ .

The action functional for braided electrodynamics follows from the braided homotopy Maurer–Cartan action functional for
\mathscr{L}^{\star}
which is given by

S_{\star}(\mathcal{A})=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\langle{\cal A},\ell_{1}({\cal A})\rangle_{\star}-\tfrac{1}{6}\,\langle{\cal A},\ell^{\star}_{2}({\cal A},{\cal A})\rangle_{\star}=:S_{0}(\mathcal{A})+S_{\rm int}(\mathcal{A})\ .

(3.20)

Inserting the corresponding brackets with the cyclic inner product (
3.17
) gives

S_{\star}(\mathcal{A})=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,\frac{1}{2}\,F\wedge_{\star}*_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,F-\frac{1}{12}\,\bar{\psi}\star V\wedge_{\star}\big(D^{\mbox{\tiny L}}\psi+D^{\mbox{\tiny R}}\psi\big)\ .

(3.21)

Since
F={\rm d}A
and one star-product can be removed under the integral, the action functional for the gauge field
A
is just the usual classical Maxwell action functional.

It is easy to check that
S_{\star}(\mathcal{A})
is gauge invariant, and also real for a Hermitian twist. Explicitly, using the braided Leibniz rule
one can easily check that

\displaystyle\delta^{\star}_{c}(\bar{\psi}\star V\wedge_{\star}D^{\mbox{\tiny L,R}}\psi)

\displaystyle=\delta^{\star}_{c}\bar{\psi}\star V\wedge_{\star}D^{\mbox{\tiny L,R}}\psi+\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi})\star V\wedge_{\star}\delta^{\star}_{\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(c)}D^{\mbox{\tiny L,R}}\psi

\displaystyle=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi})\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(c)\star V\wedge_{\star}D^{\mbox{\tiny L,R}}\psi

\displaystyle\quad\,+{\mathrm{i}}\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi})\star\big(\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(c)\star V-V\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(c)\big)\wedge_{\star}D^{\mbox{\tiny L,R}}\psi

\displaystyle\quad\,+{\mathrm{i}}\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi})\star V\wedge_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(c)\star D^{\mbox{\tiny L,R}}\psi\ =\ 0\ .

Compared to standard noncommutative electrodynamics with star-gauge symmetry, one direct consequence of our model is the absence of photon self-interaction vertices. Another is the curtailing of the problem of charge quantization
[
43
]
.

Looking more closely at the equations of motion
F_{A}^{\star}=0
, we immediately observe the conservation law

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,e\,\delta\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big(\bar{\psi}\star V\star\psi+\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi})\star V\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\psi)\big)=\partial_{\mu}J_{\star}^{\mu}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\approx}}\ 0

for the braided matter current

\displaystyle\quad J_{\star}^{\mu}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,e\,\big(\bar{\psi}\star\gamma^{\mu}\,\psi+\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi})\star\gamma^{\mu}\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\psi)\big)\ .

(3.22)

The corresponding conserved charge is

Q_{B}^{\star}=\int_{B}\,{\rm d}B\ J_{\star}^{0}=\frac{e}{2}\,\int_{B}\,{\rm d}B\ \big(\psi^{\dagger}\star\psi+\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\psi^{\dagger})\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\psi)\big)

(3.23)

for a spatial volume
B\subseteq\mathbbm{R}^{3}
at fixed time. From the current conservation
\partial_{\mu}J_{\star}^{\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\approx}}0
and Gauss’ theorem it follows that

\frac{{\rm d}Q_{B}^{\star}}{{\rm d}t}-\int_{\Sigma}\,{\rm d}\vec{\Sigma}\cdot\vec{J}_{\star}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle A}}{{\approx}}\ 0\ ,

where
\Sigma=\partial B
with oriented area form
{\rm d}\vec{\Sigma}
and
\vec{J}_{\star}=J_{\star}^{i}\,\partial_{i}
is the spatial current; in particular, if
B
is large enough to have no current flux through
\Sigma
, then the charge
Q_{B}^{\star}
is a constant of the motion.

Although our choice of twist is compatible with the cyclic inner product (
3.17
), the second term in (
3.23
) has a non-trivial contribution to the conserved charge, because the integration in (
3.23
) is only taken over the three-dimensional spatial volume
B
. Hence
Q_{B}^{\star}
generally differs from the electric charge not only in the classical theory but also in the standard noncommutative theory. Only when
B
is closed or
B=\mathbbm{R}^{3}
, and the time component of the twist vanishes (e.g.
\theta^{0i}=0
as in Section
2.3
), do we recover the usual electric charge.

The conserved matter current (
3.22
) also follows from the braided Noether identity in the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra description. Let us show this explicitly. The braided Noether identity
{\mathsf{d}}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\star}F_{\mathcal{A}}^{\star}=0
in
\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})[[\nu]]
is given by

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(F^{\star}_{\cal A})+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\ell_{2}^{\star}(F^{\star}_{\cal A},{\cal A})-\ell_{2}^{\star}({\cal A},F^{\star}_{\cal A})\big)+\tfrac{1}{4}\,\big(\ell_{2}^{\star}(\ell^{\star}_{2}({\cal A},{\cal A}),{\cal A})-\ell_{2}^{\star}({\cal A},\ell^{\star}_{2}({\cal A},{\cal A}))\big)=0\ .

Inserting the corresponding brackets, we find that most of the terms cancel and we are left with

\tfrac{1}{e}\,\delta F^{\star}_{A}-\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}}{12}\,e\,\delta\ast_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big(\bar{\psi}\star V\star\psi+\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi})\star V\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\psi)\big)=0\ .

As previously, we understand this as expressing the weak conservation of the electric matter current, leading to the conserved charge (
3.23
).

The description of the one-form magnetic symmetry in braided electrodynamics evidently follows the classical discussion from Section
3.1
with no changes.

4
Braided quantum electrodynamics

4.1
Braided Batalin–Vilkovisky functional

Throughout this section we will work with the Moyal–Weyl twist (
2.26
) for definiteness. Expanding the action functional (
3.21
) in the coordinate basis we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\star}(\mathcal{A})&=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,{\rm d}^{4}x\ \Big(-\frac{1}{4}\,F^{\mu\nu}\star F_{\mu\nu}+\bar{\psi}\star\big({\mathrm{i}}\,\not{\partial}-m\big)\,\psi\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt+\frac{e}{2}\,\big(\bar{\psi}\star A_{\mu}\,\gamma^{\mu}\star\psi+\bar{\psi}\star\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(A_{\mu})\,\gamma^{\mu}\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\psi)\big)\Big)\ ,\end{split}

(4.1)

where we gave the fermion field
\psi
a mass
m
in order to avoid spurious infrared divergences in the following. This action functional follows from the action functional (
3.21
) by assigning a non-zero mass term to the fermion field
\psi
.

In contrast to the scalar field theory of Section
2.4
, the BV action functional here differs from the classical action functional (
4.1
) by terms involving ghosts and antifields, due to the local
\mathsf{U}(1)
gauge symmetry of the theory. It can be computed by promoting
c\in L^{0}
to a ghost field and inserting the ‘superfield’
c+\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{A}^{+}+c^{+}
, regarded as an element of the shifted vector space
L[1]
, into the braided Maurer–Cartan action functional (
3.20
). Using the non-zero brackets from (
3.10
) and (
3.16
), together with the cyclic inner products from (
3.17
), we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}(c,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{+})&=S_{\star}(\mathcal{A})+\langle\mathcal{A}^{+},\ell_{1}(c)\rangle_{\star}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\langle c,\ell_{2}^{\star}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{+})+\ell_{2}^{\star}(\mathcal{A}^{+},\mathcal{A})\rangle_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=S_{\star}(\mathcal{A})+\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,{\rm d}^{4}x\ \Big(\frac{1}{e}\,\partial_{\mu}c\star A^{+\mu}+\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{2}\,c\star\big(\bar{\psi}^{+}\star\psi+\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi}^{+})\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\psi)\\
&\hskip 227.62204pt-\bar{\psi}\star\psi^{+}-\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi})\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\psi^{+})\big)\Big)\ .\end{split}

Since the action functional (
4.1
) is invariant under the local (braided)
\mathsf{U}(1)
gauge symmetry, we have to perform gauge fixing before quantization. In the present case this alters only the free part of the gauge sector, so we may follow the standard treatment. In the BV formalism it is implemented by extending the field content of the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathscr{L}^{\star}
with the antighost
\bar{c}\in\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})[-2]
of the ghost field
c\in\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})
, together with its antifield
\bar{c}^{+}\in\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})[-1]
, and a Nakanishi–Lautrup multiplier field
b\in\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})[-1]
along with its antifield
b^{+}\in\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})[-2]
.

This extends the cochain complex (
3.12
) to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})[-2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})[-3]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{matrix}\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}},\mathscr{S})[-1]\\[4.30554pt]
\oplus\\[4.30554pt]
\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}},\mathscr{S})[-1]\end{matrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{matrix}\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}},\mathscr{S})[-2]\\[4.30554pt]
\oplus\\[4.30554pt]
\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}},\mathscr{S})[-2]\end{matrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{matrix}\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})[-1]\\[4.30554pt]
\oplus\\[4.30554pt]
\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})[-1]\end{matrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{matrix}\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})[-2]\\[4.30554pt]
\oplus\\[4.30554pt]
\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})[-2]\end{matrix}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{1}{e}\,{\rm d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta\,{\rm d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{1}{e}\,\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ {\ \Big(\begin{matrix}0\!\!&\!\!{\mathrm{i}}\,\overleftarrow{\not{\partial}+m}\\[-0.90417pt]
{\mathrm{i}}\,\not{\partial}-m\!\!&\!\!0\end{matrix}\Big)\ }\ }
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ {\ \frac{1}{2}\,\big(\begin{matrix}0\!\!&\!\!-1\\[-3.01389pt]
1\!\!&\!\!0\end{matrix}\big)\ }\ }

where the extended pairs are
(b,\bar{c}^{+})
and
(b^{+},\bar{c})
in degrees
1
and
2
, respectively. This complex has the same cohomology
H^{\bullet}(L)
as (
3.12
). There are no further non-zero higher brackets, while the additional non-trivial inner products are
\langle(b,\bar{c}^{+})\,,\,(b^{+},\bar{c})\rangle_{\star}=\int_{{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x\ b\star b^{+}-\bar{c}^{+}\star\bar{c}
. The corresponding superfield Maurer–Cartan action functional shifts the BV functional to
S_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}-\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,{\rm d}^{4}x\ b\star\bar{c}^{+}
.

A general
R_{\xi}
gauge, for some gauge parameter
\xi\in\mathbbm{R}

[
46
]
, is then implemented by applying the isometry of
\mathscr{L}^{\star}
defined as
(A^{+},b^{+},\bar{c}^{+})\mapsto\big(A^{+}+\mathrm{d}\bar{c},b^{+}-\frac{\xi}{2}\,\bar{c},\bar{c}^{+}-\delta A-\frac{\xi}{2}\,b\big)
in the shifted BV functional. This yields the one-parameter family of gauge fixed BV action functionals

\displaystyle\begin{split}S^{{\rm gf},\xi}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}(b,c,\bar{c},\bar{c}^{+},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{+})&=S_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}(c,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{+})-\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,{\rm d}^{4}x\ \frac{1}{e}\,\bar{c}\star\square\,c-\frac{\xi}{2}\,b\star b-b\star\delta A+b\star\bar{c}^{+}\ .\end{split}

(4.2)

Note that the ghost field
c
completely decouples from the gauge field
A
due to the abelian nature of the gauge symmetry.

4.2
Braided homological perturbation theory

As in Section
2.4
, we quantize this theory by applying braided homological perturbation theory to compute correlation functions, following
[
5
,
9
,
11
]
.

Propagators.

In order to transfer the homotopy algebra structure to the cohomology
H^{\bullet}(\mathscr{L}^{\star})
of the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathscr{L}^{\star}
of electrodynamics from Section
3.3
, we need to specify a
U\mathbbm{R}^{3}
-equivariant projection
{\sf p}:L\to H^{\bullet}(L)
of degree
0
and a
U\mathbbm{R}^{3}
-invariant contracting homotopy
{\sf h}:L\to L
of degree
-1
. From (
3.12
) it follows that the cochain complex underlying
H^{\bullet}(\mathscr{L}^{\star})
is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker({\rm d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\frac{\ker(\delta\,{\rm d})}{{\rm im}({\rm d})}[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\frac{\ker(\delta\,{\rm d})}{{\rm im}({\rm d})}[-2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker({\rm d})[-3]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\bigg(\begin{matrix}0&{\mathrm{i}}\,\overleftarrow{\not{\partial}+m}\\
{\mathrm{i}}\,\not{\partial}-m&0\end{matrix}\bigg)[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm coker}\bigg(\begin{matrix}0&{\mathrm{i}}\,\overleftarrow{\not{\partial}+m}\\
{\mathrm{i}}\,\not{\partial}-m&0\end{matrix}\bigg)[-2]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

where we used the fact that the codifferential
\delta
is the Hodge adjoint of the differential
{\rm d}
.

The same arguments that we gave in Section
2.4
carry through to the present case to show that, for the purposes of computing correlation functions of the physical fields, one should take the trivial projections

\displaystyle{\sf p}^{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}=0={\sf p}^{\textrm{\tiny$(2)$}}

to the vacua
\mathcal{A}=0=\mathcal{A}^{+}
; that is, the kernel and cokernel of the Maxwell operator
\delta\,{\rm d}
and of the massive Dirac operators
{\mathrm{i}}\,\not{\partial}\pm m
become trivial on Wick rotation to Euclidean signature. Of course, this is not true for the exterior differential
{\rm d}
, and we take the remaining components to be the natural projections
{\sf p}^{\textrm{\tiny$(0)$}},{\sf p}^{\textrm{\tiny$(3)$}}:\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})\to\ker({\rm d})
to the space of constant functions.

For the components of the contracting homotopy
{\sf h}^{\textrm{\tiny$(k)$}}:L^{k}\to L^{k-1}
, which satisfy
\ell_{1}\circ{\sf h}^{\textrm{\tiny$(k)$}}=\mathrm{id}_{L^{k}}
, we consider the massless Feynman propagator
\mathsf{D}:\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})\to\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})
given by

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}=\frac{1}{\square}\qquad\mbox{with}\quad\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(k)=-\frac{1}{k^{2}}\ ,

where we indicated the action of the Green operator on plane waves of the form
{\mathrm{e}}^{\,\pm\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k\cdot x}
. We extend the massless propagator to a map
\mathsf{D}:\Omega^{1}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})\to\Omega^{1}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})
by using the
\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})
-module structure of the space of one-forms
\Omega^{1}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})
.

The massive spinor Feynman propagator
\mathsf{S}:\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}},\mathscr{S}\oplus\mathscr{S})\to\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}},\mathscr{S}\oplus\mathscr{S})
acts on fermion antifields as

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}\bigg(\begin{matrix}\bar{\psi}^{+}\\
\psi^{+}\end{matrix}\bigg)(x)=-\frac{1}{\square+m^{2}}\,\bigg(\begin{matrix}0&{\mathrm{i}}\,\not{\partial}+m\\
{\mathrm{i}}\,\overleftarrow{\not{\partial}-m}&0\end{matrix}\bigg)\bigg(\begin{matrix}\bar{\psi}^{+}\\
\psi^{+}\end{matrix}\bigg)(x)=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,{\rm d}^{4}y\ \int_{k}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,k\cdot(x-y)}\ \tilde{\mathsf{S}}(k)\,\bigg(\begin{matrix}\bar{\psi}^{+}(y)\\
\psi^{+}(y)\end{matrix}\bigg)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{S}}(k)=\frac{1}{k^{2}-m^{2}}\,\bigg(\begin{matrix}0&\not{k}+m\\
\overleftarrow{\not{k}-m}&0\end{matrix}\bigg)=:\bigg(\begin{matrix}0&\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(k)\\
\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}(k)&0\end{matrix}\bigg)\ ,

with
\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}(-k)=-\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(k)
, and we defined
\overleftarrow{(\not{k}-m)}\,\bar{\psi}^{+}:=\bar{\psi}^{+}\,(\not{k}-m)
.

Then the non-zero components of the contracting homotopy are

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\sf h}^{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}=\big(\begin{matrix}e\,\mathsf{D}\circ\delta&0\end{matrix}\big)&:L^{1}\longrightarrow L^{0}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
{\sf h}^{\textrm{\tiny$(2)$}}=\bigg(\begin{matrix}\mathsf{D}\circ{\sf\Pi}&0\\
0&\mathsf{S}\end{matrix}\bigg)&:L^{2}\longrightarrow L^{1}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
{\sf h}^{\textrm{\tiny$(3)$}}=\bigg(\begin{matrix}e\,{\rm d}\circ\mathsf{D}\\
0\end{matrix}\bigg)&:L^{3}\longrightarrow L^{2}\ ,\end{split}

(4.3)

where
{\sf\Pi}:\Omega^{1}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})\to\Omega^{1}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})
is the projector onto the image of the Maxwell operator
\delta\,{\rm d}
with eigenvalues

\displaystyle\tilde{\sf\Pi}_{\mu\nu}(k)=\eta_{\mu\nu}-\frac{k_{\mu}\,k_{\nu}}{k^{2}}

when acting on plane waves of the form
{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k\cdot x}\,{\rm d}x^{\mu}
. Explicitly

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\sf h}^{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}(\mathcal{A})=e\,\frac{\partial_{\mu}}{\square}\,A^{\mu}\quad,\quad{\sf h}^{\textrm{\tiny$(2)$}}(\mathcal{A}^{+})=\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\square}\,\Big(\eta_{\mu\nu}-\frac{\partial_{\mu}\,\partial_{\nu}}{\square}\Big)\,A^{+\nu}\,{\rm d}x^{\mu}\\[8.61108pt]
\displaystyle-\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\not{\partial}+m}{\square+m^{2}}\,\psi^{+}\\[8.61108pt]
\displaystyle-\bar{\psi}^{+}\,\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\overset{\leftarrow}{\not{\partial}}-m}{\square+m^{2}}\end{pmatrix}\quad,\quad{\sf h}^{\textrm{\tiny$(3)$}}(c^{+})=\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle e\,\frac{\partial_{\mu}}{\square}\,c^{+}\,{\rm d}x^{\mu}\\[8.61108pt]
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}\end{split}

for
\mathcal{A}\in L^{1}
,
\mathcal{A}^{+}\in L^{2}
and
c^{+}\in L^{3}
.

The propagator
\mathsf{D}
is the right inverse of the differential that appears in the ghost field kinetic term of (
4.2
). Gauge fixing is implemented by setting
{\sf h}^{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}(\mathcal{A})=0
. By our choice of representatives of the cohomology in (
4.3
), this corresponds to imposing the Lorenz gauge condition
\delta A=\partial_{\mu}A^{\mu}=0
on the gauge fields
A\in\Omega^{1}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})
. Different gauge choices correspond to different choices of contracting homotopy, or equivalently different choices of propagators in the field theory. In particular, from (
4.2
) it follows that, after eliminating the auxiliary field
b
through its equation of motion, a general
R_{\xi}
gauge corresponds to the choice of photon propagator with

\displaystyle\tilde{\sf\Pi}_{\mu\nu}(k)=\eta_{\mu\nu}-(1-\xi)\,\frac{k_{\mu}\,k_{\nu}}{k^{2}}\ .

Only two choices are associated with projectors: the Landau gauge
\xi=0
, which projects onto the image of
\delta\,{\rm d}
as above, and the Feynman gauge
\xi=1
, which projects onto the kernel of
\delta\,{\rm d}
.

As in Section
2.4
, we extend the projection
{\sf p}
and the contracting homotopy
{\sf h}
to maps
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{H}
on the braided symmetric algebra
{\mathrm{Sym}}_{\mathcal{R}}L[2]
of polynomial observables of electrodynamics. Again on observables involving solely physical fields
\mathcal{A}\in L^{1}
, only
\mathsf{P}(1)=1
is non-trivial. The role of
\mathsf{H}
is to insert propagators in correlation functions of the braided quantum field theory, which we depict diagrammatically as shown in Figure
3
. On the other hand, the braided BV Laplacian
{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}
implements the braided Wick theorem in braided homological perturbation theory.

Figure 3:
From top to bottom: The ghost, spinor and photon propagators.

Vertices.

We now proceed to construct the interaction vertices that need to be inserted. We start by introducing the basis of plane waves
\mathtt{e}_{k}
for
L^{0}
and its dual basis
\mathtt{e}^{k}
for
L^{3}
as in Section
2.4
. To construct corresponding bases for
L^{1}
and
L^{2}
, we first identify the space of one-forms
\Omega^{1}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})\subset L^{1}
with
({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})^{*}\otimes\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})
. A gauge field
A\in\Omega^{1}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})
can then be expanded as
11
11

11

Throughout we omit tensor products among basis elements from different vector spaces for simplicity, e.g. we abbreviate
\mathtt{v}^{\mu}\otimes\mathtt{e}_{k}
by
\mathtt{v}^{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}_{k}
, and so on.

A=\int_{k}\,a_{\mu}(k)\,\mathtt{v}^{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}_{k}
where
\mathtt{v}^{\mu}
are covectors of the standard basis for
({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})^{*}
, and the Lorenz gauge fixing condition imposes the constraint
k^{\mu}\,a_{\mu}(k)=0
on the photon polarization vectors. Similarly, we identify
\Omega^{1}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})\subset L^{2}
with
{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\otimes\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})
and expand the antifields
A^{+}\in\Omega^{1}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})
as
A^{+}=\int_{k}\,a^{+\mu}(k)\,\mathtt{v}_{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k}
, where
\mathtt{v}_{\mu}
are vectors of a dual basis for
{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}
in the sense that

\displaystyle\langle\mathtt{v}^{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}_{k},\mathtt{v}_{\nu}\,\mathtt{e}^{p}\rangle_{\star}=\delta_{\nu}^{\mu}\,\langle\mathtt{e}_{k},\mathtt{e}^{p}\rangle_{\star}\ .

For the fermion fields
(\psi,\bar{\psi})
, we identify the vector space
\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}},\mathscr{S}\oplus\mathscr{S})\subset L^{1}
with the space
\big(\mathscr{S}\,\otimes\,\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})\big)\oplus\big(\mathscr{S}\,\otimes\,\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})\big)
and basis
(\mathtt{u}^{s},\bar{\mathtt{u}}^{s})
,
s=1,2,3,4
for
\mathscr{S}\oplus\mathscr{S}
. A Dirac spinor can then be expanded as
\psi=\int_{k}\,u_{s}(k)\,\mathtt{u}^{s}\,\mathtt{e}_{k}
, while a Dirac adjoint spinor is expanded as
\bar{\psi}=\int_{k}\,\bar{v}_{s}(k)\,\bar{\mathtt{u}}^{s}\,\mathtt{e}_{k}
. For the antifields
(\bar{\psi}^{+},\psi^{+})
, we identify
\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}},\mathscr{S}\oplus\mathscr{S})\subset L^{2}
with the space
\big(\mathscr{S}\,\otimes\,\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})\big)\oplus\big(\mathscr{S}\,\otimes\,\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})\big)
and dual basis
(\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s},\mathtt{u}_{s})
for
\mathscr{S}\oplus\mathscr{S}
. The non-zero cyclic pairings among the spinor bases are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\bar{\mathtt{u}}^{s}\,\mathtt{e}_{k},\mathtt{u}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p}\rangle_{\star}=\delta_{s^{\prime}}^{s}\,\langle\mathtt{e}_{k},\mathtt{e}^{p}\rangle_{\star}=\langle\mathtt{u}^{s}\,\mathtt{e}_{k},\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p}\rangle_{\star}\ .\end{split}

With these conventions we introduce the contracted coordinate functions
{\bm{\xi}}\in({\mathrm{Sym}}_{\mathcal{R}}L[2])\otimes L[[\nu]]
as the degree
1
elements

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\bm{\xi}}&=\int_{k}\,\big(\mathtt{e}_{k}\otimes\mathtt{e}^{k}+\mathtt{e}^{k}\otimes\mathtt{e}_{k}+\mathtt{v}^{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}_{k}\otimes\mathtt{v}_{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k}+\mathtt{v}_{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k}\otimes\mathtt{v}^{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}_{k}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt+\mathtt{u}^{s}\,\mathtt{e}_{k}\otimes\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k}+\bar{\mathtt{u}}^{s}\,\mathtt{e}_{k}\otimes\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k}\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt+\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k}\otimes\mathtt{u}^{s}\,\mathtt{e}_{k}+\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k}\otimes\bar{\mathtt{u}}^{s}\,\mathtt{e}_{k}\big)\ .\end{split}

(4.4)

Using the braided Maurer–Cartan action functional (
3.20
) and the extended cyclic braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure on
({\mathrm{Sym}}_{\mathcal{R}}L[2])\otimes L[[\nu]]
, we introduce the interacting part of the BV action functional
\mathcal{S}_{\rm int}\in{\mathrm{Sym}}_{\mathcal{R}}L[2]
as the degree
0
element

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\rm int}:=-\tfrac{1}{6}\,\langle\!\!\langle{{\bm{\xi}}},{\bm{\ell}}^{\star}_{2}({{\bm{\xi}}},{{\bm{\xi}}})\rangle\!\!\rangle_{\star}\ .

(4.5)

In this paper we are solely interested in correlation functions of
physical
fields
\mathcal{A}\in L^{1}
, so we can simplify the computation by keeping only those terms of (
4.4
) involving antifields which will generate non-zero braided Wick contractions and antibrackets with elements of
L^{1}
. Likewise, from (
4.2
) it follows that we may drop all ghost terms from (
4.4
). In other words, for our purposes it suffices to compute (
4.5
) when evaluated on the element

\displaystyle{\bm{\xi}}^{\rm phys}=\int_{k}\,\big(\mathtt{v}_{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k}\otimes\mathtt{v}^{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}_{k}+\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k}\otimes\mathtt{u}^{s}\,\mathtt{e}_{k}+\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k}\otimes\bar{\mathtt{u}}^{s}\,\mathtt{e}_{k}\big)\ .

With this simplification in mind, we use the non-zero
2
-brackets from (
3.16
) and cyclic inner products from (
3.17
), together with braided cyclicity of the extended inner product, and perform analogous calculations to those of Section
2.4
. Additionally, we keep in mind that the contracted coordinate functions
{{\bm{\xi}}}
are
U\mathfrak{v}
-invariant elements of
({\mathrm{Sym}}_{\mathcal{R}}L[2])\otimes L[[\nu]]
and hence all appearances of
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrices in the properties
of the extended brackets
{\bm{\ell}}_{n}
and of the extended pairing
\langle\!\!\langle\>,\>\rangle\!\!\rangle_{\star}
disappear when evaluated on tensor powers of the
U\mathfrak{v}
-invariant element
{{\bm{\xi}}}
. This yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{S}_{\rm int}&=-\frac{1}{6}\,\int_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\,3\,\langle\!\!\langle\mathtt{v}_{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{1}}\otimes\mathtt{v}^{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}_{k_{1}}\,,\,{\bm{\ell}}_{2}^{\star}(\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\otimes\mathtt{u}^{s}\,\mathtt{e}_{k_{2}},\mathtt{u}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\otimes\bar{\mathtt{u}}^{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}_{k_{3}})\\
&\hskip 199.16928pt+{\bm{\ell}}_{2}^{\star}(\mathtt{u}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\otimes\bar{\mathtt{u}}^{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}_{k_{3}},\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\otimes\mathtt{u}^{s}\,\mathtt{e}_{k_{2}})\rangle\!\!\rangle_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=\frac{1}{2}\,\int_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\,2\,\langle\!\!\langle\mathtt{v}_{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{1}}\otimes\mathtt{v}^{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}_{k_{1}},\big(\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}})\big)\otimes\ell_{2}^{\star}\big(\mathtt{u}^{s}\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\mathtt{e}_{k_{2}}),\bar{\mathtt{u}}^{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}_{k_{3}}\big)\rangle\!\!\rangle_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=\int_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{2}\cdot\theta\,k_{3}}\,\langle\!\!\langle\mathtt{v}_{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{1}}\otimes\mathtt{v}^{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}_{k_{1}},(\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}})\otimes\ell_{2}^{\star}(\mathtt{u}^{s}\,\mathtt{e}_{k_{2}},\bar{\mathtt{u}}^{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}_{k_{3}})\rangle\!\!\rangle_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=\int_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{2}\cdot\theta\,k_{3}}\,\mathtt{v}_{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{1}}\odot_{\star}\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}})\,\langle\mathtt{v}^{\mu}\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\mathtt{e}_{k_{1}}),\ell_{2}^{\star}(\mathtt{u}^{s}\,\mathtt{e}_{k_{2}},\bar{\mathtt{u}}^{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}_{k_{3}})\rangle_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=-e\,\int_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\sum\limits_{a<b}\,k_{a}\cdot\theta\,k_{b}}\,\mathtt{v}_{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{1}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\,\langle\mathtt{v}^{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}_{k_{1}},\mathtt{v}_{\nu}\,(\bar{\mathtt{u}}^{s^{\prime}}\,\gamma^{\nu}\,\mathtt{u}^{s})\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\mathtt{e}_{k_{3}})\star\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\mathtt{e}_{k_{2}})\rangle_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=-e\,\int_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\sum\limits_{a<b}\,k_{a}\cdot\theta\,k_{b}}\,(\bar{\mathtt{u}}^{s^{\prime}}\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\mathtt{u}^{s})\ \mathtt{v}_{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{1}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\ \langle\mathtt{e}_{k_{1}},\mathtt{e}_{k_{2}}\star\mathtt{e}_{k_{3}}\rangle_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=:\int_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\,V^{\mu;s,s^{\prime}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\ \mathtt{v}_{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{1}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\ .\end{split}

The photon-fermion vertex is given by

\displaystyle V^{\mu;s,s^{\prime}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=-e\,(\gamma^{\mu})^{ss^{\prime}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{2}\,\sum\limits_{a<b}\,k_{a}\cdot\theta\,k_{b}}\ (2\pi)^{4}\,\delta(k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3})\ ,

(4.6)

where
(\gamma^{\mu})^{ss^{\prime}}:=\bar{\mathtt{u}}^{s}\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\mathtt{u}^{s^{\prime}}
are the Dirac matrix elements in our chosen basis for the complex spinor representation
\mathscr{S}
. Its diagrammatic representation is shown in Figure
4
. Surprisingly, the result (
4.6
) is different from the vertex that follows from a naive application of standard Feynman rules to the action (
4.1
),
[
32
]
. The naive application of standard Feynman rules results in a vertex with a noncommutative contribution

\cos\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,k_{2}\cdot\theta\,k_{3}\big)
. This vertex is completely (strictly) symmetric under interchange of any pair of momenta, as well as under reflection
k_{a}\to-k_{a}
of all three momenta, while the vertex (
4.6
) has braided symmetry similarly to (
2.49
) and (
2.50
).

Note that (
4.6
) is the same vertex as in the standard noncommutative QED with star-gauge symmetry, where the noncommutative correction to the vertex also consists of solely a phase factor in the momenta. Unlike the standard noncommutative QED, here there are no three-photon or four-photon vertices and no photon-ghost vertex, as anticipated from (
4.2
).

Figure 4:
Diagrammatic representation of the photon-fermion vertex.

Correlation functions.

The correlation functions of braided QED are now defined and computed analogously to Section
2.4
by the formula

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{\mathcal{A}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{n}}^{\star}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})&=\langle 0|{\rm T}[\mathcal{A}_{1}(x_{1})\star\cdots\star\mathcal{A}_{n}(x_{n})]|0\rangle_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
:\!&=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}\,\mathsf{P}\big(({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}+\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},-\}_{\star}\,\mathsf{H})^{p}(\delta^{\mathcal{A}_{1}}_{x_{1}}\odot_{\star}\cdots\odot_{\star}\delta^{\mathcal{A}_{n}}_{x_{n}})\big)\ ,\end{split}

(4.7)

where
\delta_{x_{a}}^{\mathcal{A}_{a}}\in L^{2}[2]
are again Dirac distributions supported at the insertion points
x_{a}\in\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}
of the chosen physical fields
\mathcal{A}_{a}\in L^{1}
. With our conventions these distributions are given by

\displaystyle\delta_{x_{a}}^{A_{\mu_{a}}}(x):=\mathtt{v}_{\mu_{a}}\,\delta(x-x_{a})\ ,\quad\delta_{x_{a}}^{\psi_{s_{a}}}(x):=\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s_{a}}\,\delta(x-x_{a})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\delta_{x_{a}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{a}}}(x):=\mathtt{u}_{s_{a}}\,\delta(x-x_{a})\ ,

for the various species of photon field
A\in\Omega^{1}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})
and fermions
(\psi,\bar{\psi}\,)\in\Omega^{0}({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}},\mathscr{S}\oplus\mathscr{S})
.

Arguing similarly to Section
2.4
, the operator
\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},-\}_{\star}
increases the symmetric algebra degree
n
by
1
. Thus if
n=2k
is even, the series (
4.7
) starts at
p=k
and a non-vanishing contribution requires an even number
2l
of photon-fermion vertex insertions; in this case
k+l
braided Wick contractions
{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}
are needed. On the other hand, if
n=2k+1
is odd then an odd number
2l+1
of vertex insertions is required together with
k+l+1
applications of
{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}
. In both instances the expansion parameter is
\kappa:=\hbar\,e^{2}
: the contribution to a
2k
-point function is weighted by
\hbar^{k}\,\kappa^{l}
and to a
2k{+}1
-point function by
\hbar^{k+1}\,e\,\kappa^{l}
. Note that only correlation functions involving equal numbers of Dirac fields
\psi
and
\bar{\psi}
are non-vanishing, as these are the only ones that will have non-trivial braided Wick expansions under iterated applications of
{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}
. This is the statement of charge conjugation symmetry of braided quantum electrodynamics.

Using this formalism one can calculate various correlation functions and also check the ultraviolet behaviour of the quantum field theory. We will analyse the photon and fermion self-energies at one-loop order in Sections
4.4
and
4.5
, respectively.

4.3
Braided Wick’s theorem for Dirac spinors

We first consider the theory of a free Dirac field. In the classical case, Wick’s theorem expresses the non-zero correlation functions as the Pfaffian of the two-point correlation matrix:

\displaystyle\langle 0|{\rm T}[\psi(1)\cdots\psi(2k)]|0\rangle^{(0)}=\frac{1}{k!\,2^{k}}\,\sum_{\sigma\in S_{2k}}\,{\rm sgn}(\sigma)\ \prod_{a=1}^{k}\,\langle 0|{\rm T}\big[\psi\big(\sigma(2a-1)\big)\,\psi\big(\sigma(2a)\big)\big]|0\rangle^{(0)}\ ,

(4.8)

where for compactness we have written in the argument of
\psi(a)
the spacetime coordinate
x_{a}
, the spinor index
s_{a}
, and a
\mathbbm{Z}_{2}
-valued index which distinguishes
\psi
from
\bar{\psi}
. The non-vanishing two-point functions are determined by the free fermion propagator

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{\hbar}\,\langle 0|{\rm T}[\psi_{s}(x)\,\bar{\psi}_{r}(y)]|0\rangle^{(0)}=\int_{k}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,k\cdot(x-y)}\ \tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{sr}^{+}(k)\ .

In the following we abbreviate the non-zero Wick contractions as

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.67578pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.18411pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.67578pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.18411pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.83192pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=9.16527pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.5125pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=8.84584pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}\psi_{a}\,\bar{\psi}_{b}:=\langle 0|{\rm T}[\psi_{s_{a}}(x_{a})\,\bar{\psi}_{s_{b}}(x_{b})]|0\rangle^{(0)}=-\mathchoice{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.50833pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.18411pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.50833pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.18411pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.33334pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=9.16527pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.33334pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=8.84584pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{}\bar{\psi}_{b}\,\psi_{a}\ .

(4.9)

Arguing similarly to Section
2.3
, in the free braided quantum field theory we expect the correlation functions to be determined by a formula analogous to (
2.27
), with the Hafnian replaced by the Pfaffian formula (
4.8
). For our later calculations we will need to carefully understand how this braided Wick expansion is implemented in homological perturbation theory, where the
2k
-point functions are computed by applying the operator
({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})^{k}
to braided symmetric products of
k
distributions of the form
\delta^{\psi_{s_{a}}}_{x_{a}}
with
k
distributions of the form
\delta^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{a}}}_{x_{a}}
. To properly implement Fermi statistics, we need to carefully define both the action of the extended contracting homotopy
\mathsf{H}
and the braided BV Laplacian
{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}
when acting on spinor fields.
12
12

12

The inclusion of fermions into the
L_{\infty}
-algebra framework is discussed in e.g.
[
44
,
45
]
. However, we could not find a detailed treatment of fermions in homological perturbation theory in the literature.
There are two important extra signs which arise in this case: an extra weight
(-1)^{a-1}
in each term of the sum (
2.37
) defining the extended contracting homotopy
\mathsf{H}
, and an extra weight
(-1)^{(a-1)+(b-1-a)}=(-1)^{b}
in each term of the sum in (
2.39
) defining the braided BV Laplacian
{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}
. The only non-trivial pairings (
3.17
) are between
\psi,\bar{\psi}
and their corresponding antifields
\bar{\psi}^{+},\psi^{+}
. This definition also fixes the antibrackets of spinor fields through the relation (
2.44
).

In the following we consider some illustrative examples of how these rules work.

Two-point functions.

Consider the free braided two-point Green’s function defined by

\displaystyle G_{\psi_{s_{1}},\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}^{\star}(x_{1},x_{2})^{(0)}=\langle 0|{\rm T}[\psi_{s_{1}}(x_{1})\star\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}(x_{2})]|0\rangle^{(0)}_{\star}:={\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\big)\ .

Applying (
2.37
) and (
2.39
) we obtain

\displaystyle G_{\psi_{s_{1}},\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}^{\star}(x_{1},x_{2})^{(0)}=\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar}{2}\,\Big(\big\langle\mathsf{S}\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\big),\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\big\rangle_{\star}-\big\langle\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}},\mathsf{S}\big(\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\big)\big\rangle_{\star}\Big)\ ,

(4.10)

where the minus sign comes from the fermionic Koszul sign rules discussed above. The first inner product gives

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathsf{S}\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\big),\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\big\rangle_{\star}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,{\rm d}^{4}x\ \delta(x-x_{2})\ \int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,{\rm d}^{4}y\ \int_{k}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,k\cdot(x-y)}\ \big(\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s_{1}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}(k)\,\mathtt{u}_{s_{2}}\big)\ \delta(y-x_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{k}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,k\cdot(x_{2}-x_{1})}\ \tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}_{s_{1}s_{2}}(k)=-\int_{k}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,k\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})}\ \tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}_{s_{1}s_{2}}(k)\ ,

where in the last step we changed integration variable
k\to-k
and used
\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{\pm}(-k)=-\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{\mp}(k)
. Similarly, the second inner product gives

\displaystyle\big\langle\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}},\mathsf{S}\big(\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\big)\big\rangle_{\star}=\int_{k}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,k\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})}\ \tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}_{s_{1}s_{2}}(k)\ .

Finally, substituting into (
4.10
) yields the free braided two-point function

\displaystyle G_{\psi_{s_{1}},\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}^{\star}(x_{1},x_{2})^{(0)}=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,\int_{k}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,k\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})}\ \tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}_{s_{1}s_{2}}(k)=\mathchoice{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.50002pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.24309pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.50002pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.24309pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.59726pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=9.04869pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.27783pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=8.72926pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}\psi_{1}\,\bar{\psi}_{2}\ ,

which is exactly the Feynman propagator for the Dirac spinor field, as anticipated.

In our BV formalism, the anticommutation relation (
4.9
) follows from the calculation

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.74307pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.24309pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.74307pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.24309pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.45142pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=9.04869pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.45142pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=8.72926pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{}\bar{\psi}_{2}\,\psi_{1}

\displaystyle=G_{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}},\psi_{s_{1}}}^{\star}(x_{2},x_{1})^{(0)}={\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,\big(\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar}{2}\,\Big(\big\langle\mathsf{S}\big(\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\big),\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\big\rangle_{\star}-\big\langle\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}},\mathsf{S}\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\big)\big\rangle_{\star}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar}{2}\,\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,{\rm d}^{4}x\ \int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,{\rm d}^{4}y\ \int_{k}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,k\cdot(x-y)}\,\Big(\big(\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s_{1}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(k)\,\mathtt{u}_{s_{2}}\big)\,\delta(y-x_{2})\,\delta(x-x_{1})

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt-\big(\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s_{1}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}(k)\,\mathtt{u}_{s_{2}}\big)\,\delta(y-x_{1})\,\delta(x-x_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,\int_{k}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,k\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})}\ \tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}_{s_{1}s_{2}}(k)=-\mathchoice{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.50002pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.24309pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.50002pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.24309pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.59726pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=9.04869pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.27783pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=8.72926pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}\psi_{1}\,\bar{\psi}_{2}\ .

Four-point functions.

Consider the free four-point Green’s function defined by

\displaystyle G^{\star}_{\psi_{s_{1}},\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}},\psi_{s_{3}},\bar{\psi}_{s_{4}}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})^{(0)}=

\displaystyle\ \langle 0|{\rm T}[\psi_{s_{1}}(x_{1})\star\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}(x_{2})\star\psi_{s_{3}}(x_{3})\star\bar{\psi}_{s_{4}}(x_{4})]|0\rangle^{(0)}_{\star}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ ({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})^{2}\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{3}}^{\psi_{s_{3}}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{4}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{4}}}\big)\ .

This is evaluated using

\displaystyle{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\big(\delta_{x_{a}}^{\psi_{s_{a}}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{b}}^{\psi_{s_{b}}}\big)=0={\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\big(\delta_{x_{a}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{a}}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{b}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{b}}}\big)

and

\displaystyle{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\big(\delta_{x_{a}}^{\psi_{s_{a}}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{b}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{b}}}\big)=\mathchoice{\lower
