width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.24309pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.74307pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.24309pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.45142pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=9.04869pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.45142pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=8.72926pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{}\bar{\psi}_{2}\,\psi_{3}\ .

(4.11)

This also agrees with the classical free fermion four-point function.

The other non-zero four-point functions can be similarly obtained. For example

\displaystyle G^{\star}_{\psi_{s_{1}},\psi_{s_{2}},\bar{\psi}_{s_{3}},\bar{\psi}_{s_{4}}}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})^{(0)}=-\mathchoice{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.50002pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=20.21056pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.50002pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=20.21056pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.59726pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=16.96742pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.27783pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=15.5855pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}\psi_{1}\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\bar{\psi}_{3})\,\mathchoice{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 15.46748pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=20.21056pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 15.46748pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=20.21056pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 12.51599pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=16.96742pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 11.13408pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=15.5855pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\psi_{2})\,\bar{\psi}_{4}+\mathchoice{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.50002pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.24309pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.50002pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.24309pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.59726pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=9.04869pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.27783pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=8.72926pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{}\psi_{1}\,\bar{\psi}_{4}\,\mathchoice{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.50002pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.24309pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 5.50002pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=10.24309pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.59726pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=9.04869pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{\lower 2.15277pt\vbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\kern 4.27783pt\hbox{\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt\lower 3.80551pt\hbox{\vrule width=8.72926pt,height=0.0pt,depth=0.50003pt}\vrule width=0.50003pt,height=0.0pt,depth=4.30554pt}\hss}}}{}\psi_{2}\,\bar{\psi}_{3}\ ,

where we simplified the last term by using the fact that the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix acts trivially on the two-point functions, analogously to (
2.29
).

4.4
Photon self-energy at one-loop

The two-point photon correlation function is obtained from (
4.7
) by setting
n=2
,
\mathcal{A}_{1}=A_{\mu}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}=A_{\nu}
. It is easy to see that the only non-vanishing contributions at one-loop order are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}G^{\star}_{A_{\mu},A_{\nu}}(x_{1},x_{2})^{(1)}&=\langle 0|{\rm T}[A_{\mu}(x_{1})\star A_{\nu}(x_{2})]|0\rangle_{\star}^{(1)}\\[4.0pt]
:\!&=({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})^{2}\,\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\,\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{A_{\mu}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{A_{\nu}}\big)\big\}_{\star}\big\}_{\star}\\
&\quad\,+{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{A_{\mu}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{A_{\nu}}\big)\big\}_{\star}\big\}_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=:\mathcal{G}^{1}_{\mu\nu}(x_{1},x_{2})+\mathcal{G}^{2}_{\mu\nu}(x_{1},x_{2})\ .\end{split}

(4.12)

We start from the non-zero inner products

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathtt{v}_{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{a}},\mathsf{D}\,\mathsf{\Pi}\big(\delta_{x_{b}}^{A_{\nu}}\big)\big\rangle_{\star}={\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{a}\cdot x_{b}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(k_{a})\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\mu\nu}(k_{a})

(4.13)

to compute the basic operation

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{A_{\mu}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{A_{\nu}}\big)\big\}_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 28.45274pt=\frac{1}{2}\,\int_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\,V^{\lambda;s,s^{\prime}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\,\big({\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{1}\cdot x_{1}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(k_{1})\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\mu\lambda}(k_{1})\,\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{A_{\nu}}\\
&\hskip 184.9429pt+{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{1}\cdot x_{2}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(k_{1})\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\lambda\nu}(k_{1})\,\delta_{x_{1}}^{A_{\mu}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\big)\ ,\end{split}

(4.14)

where we used momentum conservation to trivialize the phase factors arising from
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix insertions in the braided derivation property of the antibracket.
We will now evaluate each contribution
\mathcal{G}^{1}_{\mu\nu}(x_{1},x_{2})
and
\mathcal{G}^{2}_{\mu\nu}(x_{1},x_{2})
to (
4.12
) separately in turn.

Evaluation of
\bm{\mathcal{G}^{1}_{\mu\nu}(x_{1},x_{2})}
.

We start with another iteration of the expression (
4.14
) by applying the operator
\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},-\}_{\star}\circ\mathsf{H}
again, which generates pairings involving both photon propagators on the remaining external legs and fermion propagators for the internal loops. In this case, momentum conservation does not always trivialize
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix factors arising from the braided derivation property of the antibracket. In particular, we encounter the basic braided symmetric product relations

\displaystyle\delta_{x_{a}}^{A_{\mu}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{e}^{k_{b}}=\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(\mathtt{e}^{k_{b}})\odot_{\star}\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(\delta_{x_{a}}^{A_{\mu}})=\mathtt{e}^{k_{b}}\odot_{\star}\delta^{A_{\mu}}_{x_{a}+\theta\,k_{b}}

(4.15)

in two of the antibrackets with
\mathcal{S}_{\rm int}
coming from the second line after applying the extended contracting homotopy
\mathsf{H}
.

Since the only non-zero pairings occur between elements in complementary degrees
1
and
2
, the only non-trivial antibrackets that survive are those which come from a dual pairing generated by
\mathsf{H}
, which lowers the degree from
2
to
1
, and so always involve propagators. For example, a non-zero pairing involving the spinor basis elements is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{a}},\mathsf{S}(\mathtt{u}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{b}})\rangle_{\star}&=\int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,{\rm d}^{4}x\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{a}\cdot x}\ \int_{\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}}\,{\rm d}^{4}y\ \int_{p}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,p\cdot(x-y)}\ \big(\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(p)\,\mathtt{u}_{s^{\prime}}\big)\ {\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{b}\cdot y}\\[4.0pt]
&=(2\pi)^{4}\,\delta(k_{a}+k_{b})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}_{ss^{\prime}}(-k_{b})=-(2\pi)^{4}\,\delta(k_{a}+k_{b})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}_{ss^{\prime}}(k_{b})\ ,\end{split}

(4.16)

where we used
\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{\pm}(-k)=-\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{\mp}(k)
. Similarly, the only other non-zero spinor pairing is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\mathsf{S}(\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{b}}),\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{a}}\rangle_{\star}=-(2\pi)^{4}\,\delta(k_{a}+k_{b})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}_{s^{\prime}s}(k_{b})\ .\end{split}

(4.17)

The first term that arises after applying
\mathsf{H}
to (
4.14
) involves the non-zero antibracket

\displaystyle\big\{\mathtt{v}_{\sigma}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{r^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{3}}\,,\,\mathsf{S}(\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}})\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\odot_{\star}\delta^{A_{\nu}}_{x_{2}}\big\}_{\star}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=-\mathtt{v}_{\sigma}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\odot_{\star}\big\langle\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{r^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{3}}\,,\,\mathsf{S}(\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}})\big\rangle_{\star}\,\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\odot_{\star}\delta^{A_{\nu}}_{x_{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=(2\pi)^{4}\,\delta(p_{3}+k_{2})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{r^{\prime}s}^{-}(k_{2})\,\mathtt{v}_{\sigma}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{A_{\nu}}\ ,

and similarly for the other five terms generated.

After some calculation using momentum conservation, as well as relabellings of spinor momenta, one arrives at

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\,\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{A_{\mu}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{A_{\nu}}\big)\big\}_{\star}\big\}_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&\quad=\frac{1}{6}\,\int_{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}}\,V^{\sigma;r,r^{\prime}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\ \int_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\,V^{\lambda;s,s^{\prime}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\times\Big[(2\pi)^{4}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{1}\cdot x_{1}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(k_{1})\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\lambda\mu}(k_{1})\Big(\delta(p_{3}+k_{2})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{r^{\prime}s}^{-}(k_{2})\,\mathtt{v}_{\sigma}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\\
&\hskip 99.58464pt+{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}\,(p_{3}+k_{2})\cdot\theta\,p_{2}}\,\delta(p_{2}+k_{3})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{s^{\prime}r}^{-}(p_{2})\,\mathtt{v}_{\sigma}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{r^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{3}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\Big)\,\odot_{\star}\delta^{A_{\nu}}_{x_{2}}\\
&\hskip 71.13188pt+(2\pi)^{4}{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{1}\cdot x_{2}}\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(k_{1})\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\lambda\nu}(k_{1})\,\delta^{A_{\mu}}_{x_{1}}\\
&\hskip 128.0374pt\odot_{\star}\Big(\delta(p_{3}+k_{2})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{r^{\prime}s}^{-}(k_{2})\mathtt{v}_{\sigma}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\\
&\hskip 156.49014pt+{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}\,p_{3}\cdot\theta\,p_{2}}\,\delta(p_{2}+k_{3})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{s^{\prime}r}^{-}(p_{2})\,\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\,\mathtt{v}_{\sigma}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{r^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{3}}\Big)\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt+2\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,p_{1}\cdot x_{1}+{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{1}\cdot x_{2}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(p_{1})\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\mu\sigma}(p_{1})\,\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(k_{1})\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\lambda\nu}(k_{1})\\
&\hskip 227.62204pt\times\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{r^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{3}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\Big]\ .\end{split}

(4.18)

Finally, applying the operator
({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})^{2}
to (
4.18
) generates free four-point functions, which we evaluate using the braided Wick expansions (
2.32
) and (
4.11
). Many pairings vanish in this case.

Let us start with the last line of (
4.18
), whose Wick expansion gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}&({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})^{2}\big(\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{r^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{3}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\big)\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 56.9055pt=-\hbar^{2}\,\big(\langle\mathsf{S}(\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{r^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{3}}),\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\rangle_{\star}\,\langle\mathsf{S}(\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}),\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\rangle_{\star}\\
&\hskip 199.16928pt+\langle\mathsf{S}(\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}),\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\rangle_{\star}\,\langle\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{r^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{3}},\mathsf{S}(\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}})\rangle_{\star}\big)\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 56.9055pt=-\hbar^{2}\,(2\pi)^{8}\,\big(\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{r^{\prime}r}^{+}(p_{3})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{s^{\prime}s}^{+}(k_{3})\,\delta(p_{2}+p_{3})\,\delta(k_{2}+k_{3})\\
&\hskip 199.16928pt+\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{r^{\prime}s}^{-}(k_{2})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}_{s^{\prime}r}(k_{3})\,\delta(p_{2}+k_{3})\,\delta(k_{2}+p_{3})\big)\ .\end{split}

(4.19)

Substituting into (
4.18
) and relabelling momenta, it is straightforward to see that the contribution of the first term in the last line of (
4.19
) can be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{1}{3}\,\Big({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,e\,\int_{k_{1},p_{1}}\,(2\pi)^{4}\,\delta(p_{1})\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,p_{1}\cdot x_{1}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(p_{1})\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\mu\sigma}(p_{1})\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(\gamma^{\sigma}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(k_{1})\big)\Big)\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\times\Big({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,e\,\int_{k_{2},p_{2}}\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(\gamma^{\lambda}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(k_{2})\big)\,(2\pi)^{4}\,\delta(p_{2})\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,p_{2}\cdot x_{2}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(p_{2})\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\lambda\nu}(p_{2})\Big)\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 56.9055pt=\frac{1}{3}\,\Big({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{A_{\mu}}\big)\big\}_{\star}\Big)\,\Big({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\big(\delta_{x_{2}}^{A_{\nu}}\big)\big\}_{\star}\Big)\ ,\end{split}

(4.20)

where
\mathrm{Tr}
is the trace over spinor indices (see Appendix
B
), and the additional delta-functions have set all
\theta
-dependent quantities to unity, including the contributions from the vertex (
4.6
). This is recognised as a disconnected contribution to the photon two-point function from the fermion tadpoles at this order, as depicted diagrammatically in Figure
5
. As in the standard noncommutative QED, the tadpole contributions coincide with their commutative counterparts. Each one-point function is superficially divergent, but vanishes after cutoff regularization using
\mathrm{Tr}(\gamma^{\mu})=0
and
\int_{|k|<\Lambda}\,k^{\mu}/(k^{2}-m^{2})=0
; this is a special case of Furry’s theorem in usual QED.

Figure 5:
The disconnected (left) and connected (right) contributions to the photon two-point function at one-loop order. The photon one-point functions each vanish.

Similarly, the contribution to (
4.18
) from the second term in the last line of (
4.19
) can be easily manipulated to the form

\displaystyle-\frac{\hbar^{2}\,e^{2}}{3}\,\int_{k,p}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,p\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})}\,\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(p)^{2}\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\mu\sigma}(p)\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\lambda\nu}(p)\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}(p-k)\,\gamma^{\sigma}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(k)\,\gamma^{\lambda}\big)\ .

(4.21)

This is recognised as a contribution to the connected two-point function from the fermion bubble at this order, as depicted diagrammatically in Figure
5
. We immediately realize that there are no noncommutative contributions in this term.

For the first four terms in (
4.18
), we compute the braided Wick expansion

\displaystyle({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})^{2}\big(\mathtt{v}_{\sigma}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{A_{\nu}}\big)

\displaystyle=-\hbar^{2}\,\langle\mathtt{v}_{\sigma}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{1}},\mathsf{D}\,\mathsf{\Pi}(\delta_{x_{2}}^{A_{\nu}})\rangle_{\star}\,\langle\mathsf{S}(\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}),\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\rangle_{\star}

\displaystyle=-\hbar^{2}\,(2\pi)^{4}\,\delta(p_{2}+k_{2})\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,p_{1}\cdot x_{2}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(p_{1})\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\sigma\nu}(p_{1})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{s^{\prime}r}^{+}(k_{2})

and similarly

\displaystyle({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})^{2}\big(\mathtt{v}_{\sigma}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{r^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{A_{\nu}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt=-\hbar^{2}\,(2\pi)^{4}\,\delta(p_{2}+k_{2})\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,p_{1}\cdot x_{2}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(p_{1})\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\sigma\nu}(p_{1})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{r^{\prime}s}^{-}(k_{2})\ ,

along with the corresponding expressions for the remaining terms with
\delta_{x_{1}}^{A_{\mu}}
. After substitution into (
4.18
) and resolving the delta-functions, we find that all four terms contribute equally and give

\displaystyle-\frac{2\,\hbar^{2}\,e^{2}}{3}\,\int_{k,p}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,p\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})}\,\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(p)^{2}\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\mu\sigma}(p)\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\lambda\nu}(p)\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}(p-k)\,\gamma^{\sigma}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(k)\,\gamma^{\lambda}\big)\ .

(4.22)

Altogether, summing (
4.21
) and (
4.22
) we arrive at

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{1}_{\mu\nu}(x_{1},x_{2})=-\hbar^{2}\,e^{2}\,\int_{k,p}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,p\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})}\

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(p)^{2}\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\mu\sigma}(p)\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\lambda\nu}(p)\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}(p-k)\,\gamma^{\sigma}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(k)\,\gamma^{\lambda}\big)\ .

(4.23)

As in (
4.21
), all noncommutative contributions vanish.

Evaluation of
\bm{\mathcal{G}^{2}_{\mu\nu}(x_{1},x_{2})}
.

Now we start by applying the operator
{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}
to (
4.14
) using

\displaystyle{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\big(\mathtt{u}_{s}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{s^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{A_{\nu}}\big)=\tfrac{2}{3}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,(2\pi)^{4}\,\delta(k_{2}+k_{3})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{s^{\prime}s}^{+}(k_{3})\ \delta_{x_{2}}^{A_{\nu}}

and the corresponding expression for the terms with
\delta_{x_{1}}^{A_{\mu}}
. Upon further application of the operator
{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\circ\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},-\}_{\star}\circ\mathsf{H}
, it is easy to see that these expressions contribute to (
4.12
) with the same momentum and spinor index structure as that which led to the disconnected contribution (
4.20
). Hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{2}_{\mu\nu}(x_{1},x_{2})=0\ .

(4.24)

Vacuum polarization.

From (
4.23
) and (
4.24
) it follows that the one-loop photon two-point function (
4.12
) is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}G^{\star}_{A_{\mu},A_{\nu}}(x_{1},x_{2})^{(1)}=-\hbar^{2}\,e^{2}\,\int_{k,p}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,p\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})}\ &\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(p)^{2}\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\mu\sigma}(p)\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\lambda\nu}(p)\\
&\times\,\mathrm{Tr}\big(\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}(p-k)\,\gamma^{\sigma}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(k)\,\gamma^{\lambda}\big)\ .\end{split}

(4.25)

As before, we relate the all orders photon two-point function to the dressed photon propagator through the self-energy
\Pi^{\mu\nu}_{\star}(p)
by

\displaystyle G^{\star}_{A_{\mu},A_{\nu}}(x_{1},x_{2})=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,\int_{(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})^{*}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,p\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})}\ \bigg(\frac{1}{p^{2}\,\big(\eta-(1-\xi)\,\frac{p\,\otimes\,p}{p^{2}}\big)^{-1}-\Pi_{\star}(p)}\bigg)_{\mu\nu}\ .

At order
e^{2}
, the result (
4.25
) leads to

\displaystyle\hskip-2.84544pt\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{\hbar}\,\Pi^{\mu\nu}_{\star 2}(p)=e^{2}\,\int_{(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})^{*}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ \frac{\mathrm{Tr}\big((\not{p}-\not{k}-m)\,\gamma^{\mu}\,(\not{k}+m)\,\gamma^{\nu}\big)}{\big((p-k)^{2}-m^{2}\big)\,\big(k^{2}-m^{2}\big)}\ .

(4.26)

The vacuum polarization tensor (
4.26
) is identical to the result of the photon self-energy calculation in the standard commutative electrodynamics, with precisely the same sign and overall combinatorial factor. As in the scalar field theory of Section
2.4
, the braided Wick theorem contributes phase factors which cancel the noncommutative contributions of the interaction vertex (
4.6
).

This result is different from the result of
[
32
]
, which was based on a naive application of the conventional Feynman rules following from the action functional (
4.1
). There the noncommutative contribution of the form
\cos^{2}\big(\frac{1}{2}\,p\cdot\theta\,k\big)
appears and in this way introduces the UV/IR mixing. The result (
4.26
) suggests that the algebraic quantization using the braided BV formalism is the correct way to quantize braided field theories.

The result (
4.26
) further agrees from the corresponding contribution to the one-loop vacuum polarization in standard noncommutative QED, which coincides with that found in ordinary QED
[
43
]
: in that case each photon-fermion vertex contributes a phase factor which cancel each other in the diagram. Non-trivial corrections in the standard approach come from three more contributing diagrams due to the non-abelian nature of the star-gauge symmetry: the photon bubble, the photon tadpole and the ghost bubble. These involve non-planar diagrams which result in UV/IR mixing
[
43
,
47
]
.

4.5
Fermion self-energy at one-loop

The two-point fermion correlation function is obtained from (
4.7
) by setting
n=2
,
\mathcal{A}_{1}=\psi_{s_{1}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}=\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}
. Again the only non-vanishing contributions at one-loop order are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}G^{\star}_{\psi_{s_{1}},\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}(x_{1},x_{2})^{(1)}&=\langle 0|{\rm T}[\psi_{s_{1}}(x_{1})\star\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}(x_{2})]|0\rangle_{\star}^{(1)}\\[4.0pt]
:\!&=({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})^{2}\,\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\,\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\big)\big\}_{\star}\big\}_{\star}\\
&\quad\,+{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\,\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\,({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\big)\big\}_{\star}\big\}_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&=:\mathcal{G}_{s_{1}s_{2}}^{1}(x_{1},x_{2})+\mathcal{G}_{s_{1}s_{2}}^{2}(x_{1},x_{2})\ .\end{split}

(4.27)

The computation proceeds in a completely analogous way to that of Section
4.4
. We start from the non-zero inner products

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathsf{S}\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\big),\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{a}}\big\rangle_{\star}={\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{a}\cdot x_{1}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{s_{1}r}^{-}(k_{a})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\big\langle\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{a}},\mathsf{S}\big(\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\big)\big\rangle_{\star}={\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{a}\cdot x_{2}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{rs_{2}}^{+}(k_{a})\ ,

to get the basic operation

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\big)\big\}_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip 14.22636pt=\frac{1}{2}\,\int_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\,V^{\nu;r,r^{\prime}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\,\big(-{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{3}\cdot x_{2}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{r^{\prime}s_{2}}^{+}(k_{3})\,\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{v}_{\nu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{1}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt+{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{2}\cdot(x_{1}-\theta\,k_{3})}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{s_{1}r}^{-}(k_{2})\,\mathtt{v}_{\nu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{1}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{r^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\big)\ .\end{split}

(4.28)

Evaluation of
\bm{\mathcal{G}^{1}_{s_{1}s_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})}
.

After some algebraic manipulations, the operator
\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},-\}_{\star}\circ\mathsf{H}
applied to (
4.28
) yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hskip-8.5359pt\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\big\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},\mathsf{H}\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\big)\big\}_{\star}\big\}_{\star}\\[4.0pt]
&\hskip-8.5359pt\quad=\frac{1}{6}\,\int_{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}}\,V^{\mu;t,t^{\prime}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\ \int_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\,V^{\nu;r,r^{\prime}}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\ \Big[(2\pi)^{4}\,\delta(p_{1}+k_{1})\\
&\hskip-8.5359pt\hskip 28.45274pt\times\Big(\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(p_{1})\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\mu\nu}(p_{1})\,\mathtt{u}_{t}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{t^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{3}}\odot_{\star}\big(-{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{3}\cdot x_{2}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{r^{\prime}s_{2}}^{+}(k_{3})\,\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{1}-\theta\,k_{3}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\\
&\hskip-8.5359pt\hskip 227.62204pt+{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{2}\cdot x_{1}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{1}\cdot\theta\,k_{2}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{s_{1}r}^{-}(k_{2})\,\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{r^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\big)\\
&\hskip-8.5359pt\hskip 56.9055pt+\mathtt{v}_{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{v}_{\nu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\odot_{\star}\big({\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{2}\cdot x_{1}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,-{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{3}\cdot\theta\,p_{2}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{s_{1}r}^{-}(k_{2})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{r^{\prime}t}^{-}(p_{1})\,\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{t^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{3}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\\
&\hskip-8.5359pt\hskip 142.26378pt-{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{2}\cdot x_{2}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,-{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{3}\cdot\theta\,p_{2}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{r^{\prime}s_{2}}^{+}(k_{2})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{t^{\prime}r}^{+}(p_{1})\,\mathtt{u}_{t}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{3}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{1}-\theta\,k_{2}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\big)\Big)\\
&\hskip-8.5359pt\hskip 56.9055pt-2\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{3}\cdot x_{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\,p_{2}\cdot x_{1}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{2}\cdot\theta\,(k_{1}-p_{3})}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}_{r^{\prime}s_{2}}(k_{3})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}_{s_{1}t}(p_{2})\\
&\hskip-8.5359pt\hskip 227.62204pt\times\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{t^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{3}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{v}_{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{1}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{v}_{\nu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{1}}\Big]\ .\end{split}

(4.29)

Next we apply the operator
({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})^{2}
to each term, and use the braided Wick expansions (
2.32
) and (
4.11
).

Looking at the first term of (
4.29
), we find two contributions

\displaystyle\begin{split}&({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})^{2}\big(\mathtt{u}_{t}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{t^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{3}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{1}-\theta\,k_{2}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\big)\\[4.0pt]
&\ \ =\hbar^{2}\,(2\pi)^{4}\,\big({\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{2}\cdot x_{1}}\,\delta(p_{2}+p_{3})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{t^{\prime}t}^{+}(p_{3})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{s_{1}r}^{-}(k_{2})+{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,p_{2}\cdot(x_{1}-\theta\,k_{2})}\,\delta(p_{3}+k_{2})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}_{t^{\prime}r}(k_{2})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}_{s_{1}t}(p_{2})\big)\ .\end{split}

(4.30)

Upon substitution into (
4.29
), the spinor index structure of the first term in (
4.30
) is easily seen to give a factor

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\big(\gamma^{\mu}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(p_{3})\big)\ \tilde{\mathsf{D}}(p_{1})\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\mu\nu}(p_{1})\ \big(\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}(k_{2})\,\gamma^{\nu}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(k_{3})\big)_{s_{1}s_{2}}\ .

(4.31)

This is recognised as a contribution to the spinor two-point function from the fermion tadpole, which vanishes by an argument similar to that which led to the vanishing of the photon one-point function in Section
4.4
. The second term in (
4.30
) instead contributes to the 1PI two-point function. These are depicted diagrammatically in Figure
6
.

Figure 6:
The fermion tadpole (left) and one-particle irreducible (right) contributions to the fermion two-point function at one-loop order. The tadpole contribution vanishes.

Similary, the second term of (
4.29
) splits into a tadpole contribution (which vanishes) and a 1PI contribution. The remaining terms in (
4.29
) all yield spinor index structures that contribute only to the 1PI correlator. For example, the braided Wick expansion of the third term is given by

\displaystyle({\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H})^{2}\big(\mathtt{v}_{\mu}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{2}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{v}_{\nu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{3}}\odot_{\star}\bar{\mathtt{u}}_{t^{\prime}}\,\mathtt{e}^{p_{3}}\odot_{\star}\delta_{x_{2}}^{\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt=\hbar^{2}\,(2\pi)^{4}\,\delta(p_{2}+k_{3})\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,p_{3}\cdot x_{2}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(k_{3})\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\mu\nu}(k_{3})\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}_{t^{\prime}s_{2}}^{+}(p_{3})\ ,

and similarly for the remaining terms.

After some tedious algebraic manipulations and resolution of all delta-functions, one finds that all non-vanishing contributions are equal and result in

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{G}^{1}_{s_{1}s_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})&=-\hbar^{2}\,e^{2}\,\int_{k,p}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,p\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})}\,\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(k)\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\mu\nu}(k)\,\big(\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(p)\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}(k-p)\,\gamma^{\nu}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(p)\big)_{s_{1}s_{2}}\ .\end{split}

(4.32)

Evaluation of
\bm{\mathcal{G}^{2}_{s_{1}s_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})}
.

Now we start by applying
{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}
to (
4.28
) using

\displaystyle{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\big(\delta_{x_{1}}^{\psi_{s_{1}}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{v}_{\nu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{1}}\odot_{\star}\mathtt{u}_{r}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{2}}\big)=\tfrac{2}{3}\,{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\,{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{2}\cdot x_{1}}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,k_{1}\cdot\theta\,k_{2}}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}_{s_{1}r}(k_{2})\,\mathtt{v}_{\nu}\,\mathtt{e}^{k_{1}}\ ,

and similarly for the second term in (
4.28
). Applying the operator
{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\textrm{\tiny BV}}\,\mathsf{H}\circ\{\mathcal{S}_{\rm int},-\}_{\star}\circ\mathsf{H}
to these expressions, it is easy to see that the momentum and spinor index structures of their contributions to (
4.28
) are of the same form as that in (
4.31
), and so lead to vanishing fermion tadpole contributions. It therefore follows as in Section
4.4
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{2}_{s_{1}s_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})=0\ .

(4.33)

Self-energy.

From (
4.32
) and (
4.33
) it follows that the fermion two-point function (
4.27
) is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}G^{\star}_{\psi_{s_{1}},\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}(x_{1},x_{2})^{(1)}&=-\hbar^{2}\,e^{2}\,\int_{k,p}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,p\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})}\,\tilde{\mathsf{D}}(k)\,\tilde{\mathsf{\Pi}}_{\mu\nu}(k)\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt\times\big(\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(p)\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{-}(k-p)\,\gamma^{\nu}\,\tilde{\mathsf{S}}^{+}(p)\big)_{s_{1}s_{2}}\ .\end{split}

(4.34)

The exact two-point function defines the dressed fermion propagator through the Fourier transformation

\displaystyle G^{\star}_{\psi_{s_{1}},\bar{\psi}_{s_{2}}}(x_{1},x_{2})=-{\mathrm{i}}\,\hbar\,\int_{({\mathbbm{R}^{1,3}})^{*}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ {\mathrm{e}}^{-{\mathrm{i}}\,p\cdot(x_{1}-x_{2})}\ \bigg(\frac{1}{\not{p}-m-\Sigma_{\star}(p)}\bigg)_{s_{1}s_{2}}\ ,

where
\Sigma_{\star}(p)
is the fermion self-energy. At order
e^{2}
, the result (
4.34
) in Feynman gauge gives

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathrm{i}}}{\hbar}\,\Sigma_{\star 2}(p)=e^{2}\,\int_{(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})^{*}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\frac{\gamma^{\mu}\,(\not{p}-\not{k}-m)\,\gamma_{\mu}}{k^{2}\,\big((p-k)^{2}-m^{2}\big)}\ .

(4.35)

As with the braided correction to the photon self-energy, the fermion self-energy (
4.35
) agrees with the standard result in the commutative electrodynamics: it has no noncommutative contributions and no UV/IR mixing. It also agrees with the calculation in standard noncommutative QED, where the phases cancel between the two vertices and there is no difference from the fermion self-energy in ordinary QED
[
43
]
. In fact, also in the standard approach the fermion self-energy does not receive any noncommutative corrections and so does not display UV/IR mixing.

5
Conclusions and outlook

Braided noncommutative field theories are defined by deforming the
L_{\infty}
-structure of a field theory to a braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra
[
23
]
. Given that in the standard noncommutative quantum field theories the problem of UV/IR mixing arises, and that as a result these field theories cannot be renormalized, in this paper we started looking at the quantum properties of braided noncommutative field theories. Lacking any other currently available quantization scheme for braided fields, we exploited a braided deformation of the BV formalism
[
11
]
and homological perturbation theory to calculate quantum correlation functions. This framework is what we call braided quantum field theory.

As a first example, in Section
2.4
we discussed braided
\phi^{4}
scalar field theory. In the free field theory, correlation functions follow from the braided Wick theorem. In the commutative limit they reduce to the free correlation functions for the undeformed theory. The interaction vertex (
2.48
) is the same as in the standard noncommutative
\phi^{4}
-theory. However, the combination of the noncommutative interaction vertex with the braided Wick theorem conspire to render the interacting two-point function at one-loop order (
2.57
) free of noncommutative corrections, and in that way also free of UV/IR mixing. We also notice that, contrary to the standard noncommutative field theories, no non-planar diagrams appear. In order to decide whether or not braided scalar quantum field theories are renormalizable, one needs to extend our analysis beyond one-loop order and also to include higher-point correlation functions. In future work we plan to investigate these problems in more detail.

As a first step towards understanding gauge theories, in Section
4
we studied an example of a braided
\mathsf{U}(1)
gauge theory coupled to a Dirac fermion. We call this model braided QED. Unlike the standard noncommutative
\mathsf{U}(1)
gauge theory
[
43
,
47
]
where the photon is self-interacting and non-planar diagrams appear, braided QED is an abelian gauge theory. The photon does not interact with itself and the only interaction vertex is the photon-fermion vertex. As a consequence, no non-planar diagrams appear and the noncommutative contributions come from the interaction vertex (
4.6
) and the braided Wick theorem. Similarly to braided scalar field theory, the photon two-point function at one-loop (
4.26
) has no noncommutative contribution and it is free of the UV/IR mixing. The fermion two-point function at one-loop (
4.35
) also has no noncommutative contribution and shows no UV/IR mixing. We discussed these two-point functions in detail in Sections
4.4
and
4.5
, respectively. To gain some further insight into the ultraviolet behaviour of braided QED, the 3-point function at one-loop (vertex correction) and the corresponding beta-function at one-loop should be calculated. This is also slated into our future work plans.

The present paper clearly shows that braided noncommutative quantum field theories have better ultraviolet behaviour than the corresponding standard noncommutative field theories. We plan to extend our analysis to non-abelian gauge theories. Besides correlation functions, we also plan to discuss scattering amplitudes, following the approach of
[
4
,
5
]
but using the braided
L_{\infty}
-algebra structure of our theories and braided homological perturbation theory.

Appendix A
Drinfel’d twist deformation theory

In this appendix we briefly summarise the basic theory of Drinfel’d
twists as needed in this paper, more details can be found in
[
34
,
35
]
.

We start from the Lie
algebra of vector fields
\mathfrak{v}:=\Gamma(TM)
on a manifold
M
, which
generate infinitesimal diffeomorphisms of
M
. The enveloping algebra
U\mathfrak{v}
is naturally a cocommutative Hopf
algebra with coproduct
\Delta:U\mathfrak{v}\to U\mathfrak{v}\otimes U\mathfrak{v}
, counit

\varepsilon:U\mathfrak{v}\to\mathbbm{C}
and antipode
S:U\mathfrak{v}\to U\mathfrak{v}
defined on
generators by

\displaystyle\Delta(\xi)

\displaystyle=\xi\otimes 1+1\otimes\xi\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\Delta(1)=1\otimes 1\ ,

\displaystyle\varepsilon(\xi)

\displaystyle=0\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\varepsilon(1)=1\ ,

\displaystyle S(\xi)

\displaystyle=-\xi\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad S(1)=1\ ,

for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{v}
. The maps
\Delta
and
\varepsilon
are
extended as algebra homomorphisms, and
S
as an algebra
antihomomorphism to all of
U\mathfrak{v}
. We adopt the standard
Sweedler notation
\Delta(X)=:X_{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}\otimes X_{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}
(with summations understood) to abbreviate the
coproduct of
X\in U\mathfrak{v}
.

A Drinfel’d twist is a normalized two-cocycle of the Hopf
algebra
U\mathfrak{v}[[\nu]]
, the formal power series in a deformation parameter
\nu
with coefficients valued in
U\mathfrak{v}
. By this we mean an invertible element

\mathcal{F}\in U\mathfrak{v}[[\nu]]\otimes U\mathfrak{v}[[\nu]]
satisfying the cocycle
condition

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{12}\,(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{23}\,(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta)\mathcal{F}\ ,

where
\mathcal{F}_{12}=\mathcal{F}\otimes 1
and
\mathcal{F}_{23}=1\otimes\mathcal{F}
, together
with the normalization condition

\displaystyle(\varepsilon\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathcal{F}=1=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varepsilon)\mathcal{F}\ .

We write the power series expansion of the twist as
\mathcal{F}=:{\sf f}^{\alpha}\otimes{\sf f}_{\alpha}\in U\mathfrak{v}[[\nu]]\otimes U\mathfrak{v}[[\nu]]
, with the sum over

\alpha
understood. Then the cocycle
condition may be written in Sweedler notation as

\displaystyle{\sf f}^{\alpha}\,{\sf f}^{\beta}_{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}\otimes{\sf f}_{\alpha}\,{\sf f}^{\beta}_{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}\otimes{\sf f}_{\beta}={\sf f}^{\beta}\otimes{\sf f}^{\alpha}\,{{\sf f}_{\beta}}_{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}\otimes{\sf f}_{\alpha}\,{{\sf f}_{\beta}}_{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}\ ,

(A.1)

and the normalization condition as

\displaystyle\varepsilon({\sf f}^{\alpha})\,{\sf f}_{\alpha}=1={\sf f}^{\alpha}\,\varepsilon({\sf f}_{\alpha})\ .

(A.2)

As a consequence, the inverse twist
\mathcal{F}^{-1}=:\bar{{\sf f}}^{\alpha}\otimes\bar{{\sf f}}_{\alpha}\in U\mathfrak{v}[[\nu]]\otimes U\mathfrak{v}[[\nu]]
satisfies
similar conditions.

A Drinfel’d twist
\mathcal{F}
defines a new Hopf algebra structure on the
universal enveloping algebra
U\mathfrak{v}[[\nu]]
, which we denote by

U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
. As algebras,
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}=U\mathfrak{v}[[\nu]]
and also the
counit of
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
is the same as the counit
\varepsilon
of

U\mathfrak{v}[[\nu]]
. The new coproduct
\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}
and antipode
S_{\mathcal{F}}

of
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
are given by

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}(X):=\mathcal{F}\,\Delta(X)\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad S_{\mathcal{F}}(X):={\sf f}^{\alpha}\,S({\sf f}_{\alpha})\,S(X)\,S(\bar{{\sf f}}^{\beta})\,\bar{{\sf f}}_{\beta}\ ,

for all
X\in U\mathfrak{v}[[\nu]]
. For
X\in U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
, we adopt the Sweedler notation
\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}(X)=:X_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny$(1)$}}}\otimes X_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny$(2)$}}}
to distinguish the twisted and untwisted coproducts. The invertible
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix
{\mathcal{R}}\in U\mathfrak{v}[[\nu]]\otimes U\mathfrak{v}[[\nu]]
is induced by the twist as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}=\mathcal{F}_{21}\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}=:\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\ ,

(A.3)

where
\mathcal{F}_{21}=\tau(\mathcal{F})={\sf f}_{\alpha}\otimes{\sf f}^{\alpha}
is the twist with its legs swapped. It is easy to see that the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix is triangular, that is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{21}={\mathcal{R}}^{-1}=\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\ ,

and moreover that

\displaystyle(\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\mathrm{id}){\mathcal{R}}={\mathcal{R}}_{13}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{23}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}){\mathcal{R}}={\mathcal{R}}_{13}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{12}\ ,

where
{\mathcal{R}}_{13}=\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\otimes 1\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}
, or in Sweedler notation

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\otimes\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}=\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\otimes\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\,\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\otimes{\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(1)}}}\otimes{\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}}_{\bar{\textrm{\tiny(2)}}}=\mathsf{R}^{\beta}\,\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathsf{R}_{\beta}\ .

(A.4)

The
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix also satisfies the Yang–Baxter equation

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{12}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{13}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{23}={\mathcal{R}}_{23}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{13}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{12}\ .

(A.5)

Drinfel’d twist deformation quantization consists in twisting the enveloping
Hopf algebra
U\mathfrak{v}
to a non-cocommutative Hopf algebra
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
,
while simultaneously twisting all of its modules
[
35
]
. A
U\mathfrak{v}
-module algebra is an algebra
(\mathcal{A},\mu)
with a

U\mathfrak{v}
-action
\triangleright:U\mathfrak{v}\otimes\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
which is compatible with the algebra multiplication
via the coproduct
\Delta
, that is

\displaystyle X\mu(a\otimes b)=\mu\big(\Delta(X)(a\otimes b)\big)

for all
X\in U\mathfrak{v}
and
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
, where
\mu:\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}

is the product on
\mathcal{A}
. We will usually drop the symbol

\triangleright
to simplify the notation. This condition means in particular that
vector fields
\xi\in\mathfrak{v}
act on
(\mathcal{A},\mu)
as:

\displaystyle\xi\big(\mu(a\otimes b)\big)=\mu\big(\xi(a)\otimes b\big)+\mu\big(a\otimes\xi(b)\big)\ .

If
(\mathcal{A},\mu)
is a (left)
U\mathfrak{v}
-module algebra, then we
can deform the product
\mu
on
\mathcal{A}
by precomposing it with the
inverse of the twist

\mathcal{F}
to get a new product

\displaystyle\mu_{\star}(a\otimes b)=\mu\circ\mathcal{F}^{-1}(a\otimes b)=\mu\big(\bar{\sf f}^{\alpha}(a)\otimes\bar{\sf f}_{\alpha}(b)\big)\ ,

(A.6)

for
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
, where on the right-hand side we extend
\mu
to

\mathcal{A}[[\nu]]\otimes\mathcal{A}[[\nu]]\cong(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})[[\nu]]
by
applying it term by term to the coefficients of a formal power series.
The cocycle condition on
\mathcal{F}
guarantees that this
produces an associative star-product
\mu_{\star}
on
\mathcal{A}[[\nu]]
, and it generally defines a
noncommutative
U_{\mathcal{F}}\mathfrak{v}
-module algebra
(\mathcal{A}[[\nu]],\mu_{\star})
:

\displaystyle X\big(\mu_{\star}(a\otimes b)\big)=\mu_{\star}\big(\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}(X)(a\otimes b)\big)\ ,

for all
X\in U\mathfrak{v}
and
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
.
We denote
(\mathcal{A},\mu)
by
\mathcal{A}
and

(\mathcal{A}[[\nu]],\mu_{\star})
by
\mathcal{A}_{\star}
for brevity.

If the algebra
\mathcal{A}
is commutative,
then
\mathcal{A}_{\star}
is braided commutative: the noncommutativity of

\mathcal{A}_{\star}
is controlled by the
{\mathcal{R}}
-matrix as

\displaystyle\mu_{\star}(a\otimes b)=\mu_{\star}\big(\mathsf{R}_{\alpha}(b)\otimes\mathsf{R}^{\alpha}(a)\big)\ ,

which is easily proven by recalling that
{\mathcal{R}}=\mathcal{F}_{21}\,\mathcal{F}^{-1}
.

Appendix B
Dirac matrices in four dimensions

Let
\mathscr{S}
denote the complex spinor representation of
\mathsf{Spin}(1,3)
. The
16
-dimensional
\mathbbm{C}
-algebra
\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{S})
can be identified with the complex Clifford algebra
{\sf Cl}(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})\otimes\mathbbm{C}
. For this, we construct complex
4{\times}4
matrices
\gamma^{\mu}
with
\mu=0,1,2,3
which satisfy the anticommutation relations

\{\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}\}=2\,\eta^{\mu\nu}\,\mathbbm{1}_{4}\ .

Then a basis of the complex vector space
\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{S})
consists of the matrices
\mathbbm{1}_{4}
,
\gamma_{\mu}
,
\gamma_{\mu}\,\gamma_{\nu}
with
\mu<\nu
and
\gamma_{\mu}\,\gamma_{\nu}\,\gamma_{\lambda}
with
\mu<\nu<\lambda
, together with the chirality matrix

\gamma^{5}={\mathrm{i}}\,\gamma^{0}\,\gamma^{1}\,\gamma^{2}\,\gamma^{3}=-\tfrac{{\mathrm{i}}}{4!}\,\epsilon_{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\gamma^{\nu}\,\gamma^{\lambda}\,\gamma^{\rho}

which satisfies
\{\gamma^{5},\gamma^{\mu}\}=0
, where
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}
is the Levi–Civita symbol with the convention
\epsilon^{0123}=+1
.

For any covector
k\in(\mathbbm{R}^{1,3})^{*}
, we use the Feynman slash notation to write the corresponding element of
\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{S})
as
\not{k}:=k_{\mu}\,\gamma^{\mu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}\,k^{\mu}\,\gamma^{\nu}
. The Dirac operator can be expressed as
{\mathrm{i}}\,\not{\partial}:={\mathrm{i}}\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\partial_{\mu}
.

The Dirac representation of
\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{S})
is defined by

\gamma_{0}=\bigg(\begin{matrix}\mathbbm{1}_{2}&0\\
0&-\mathbbm{1}_{2}\end{matrix}\bigg)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\gamma^{i}=\bigg(\begin{matrix}0&\sigma^{i}\\
-\sigma^{i}&0\end{matrix}\bigg)\ ,

where
\sigma^{i}
for
i=1,2,3
are the standard
2{\times}2
Pauli spin matrices

\displaystyle\sigma^{1}=\bigg(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{matrix}\bigg)\ ,\quad\sigma^{2}=\bigg(\begin{matrix}0&-{\mathrm{i}}\\
{\mathrm{i}}&0\end{matrix}\bigg)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\sigma^{3}=\bigg(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{matrix}\bigg)

satisfying
\sigma^{i}\,\sigma^{j}=\delta^{ij}\,\mathbbm{1}_{2}+{\mathrm{i}}\,\epsilon^{0ijk}\,\sigma_{k}
. In this basis the Dirac matrices have the reality properties

\gamma_{\mu}^{\dagger}=\gamma^{0}\,\gamma_{\mu}\,\gamma^{0}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\gamma_{5}^{\dagger}=\gamma_{5}=\gamma^{5}=\gamma_{5}^{-1}\ .

One also easily derives the product formula

\gamma_{\mu}\,\gamma_{\nu}\,\gamma_{\lambda}=(\eta_{\mu\nu}\,\eta_{\lambda\rho}-\eta_{\mu\lambda}\,\eta_{\nu\rho}+\eta_{\mu\rho}\,\eta_{\nu\lambda})\,\gamma^{\rho}+{\mathrm{i}}\,\epsilon_{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}\,\gamma^{\rho}\,\gamma_{5}\ .

(B.1)

Let
\mathrm{Tr}
denote the trace in the Dirac representation. Then one easily derives the trace identities

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{Tr}(\gamma^{\mu})&=0=\mathrm{Tr}(\gamma^{\mu}\,\gamma^{\nu}\,\gamma^{\lambda})\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mathrm{Tr}(\gamma^{\mu}\,\gamma^{\nu})&=4\,\eta^{\mu\nu}\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mathrm{Tr}(\gamma^{\mu}\,\gamma^{\nu}\,\gamma^{\lambda}\,\gamma^{\rho})&=4\,\big(\eta^{\mu\nu}\,\eta^{\lambda\rho}-\eta^{\mu\lambda}\,\eta^{\nu\rho}+\eta^{\mu\rho}\,\eta^{\nu\lambda}\big)\ .\end{split}

(B.2)
